## Upgrade suggestions

### Suite-walk follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **Five guards now carry the same exemption-list SHAPE and each asserts it by hand** — an `ALLOWED_TO_*` array, a `deepEqual` pinning its contents, a loop checking each entry is still in the walk, and (in three of them) a check that each entry still NEEDS the exemption. That last one was added twice today, independently, in the same words. A `assertExemptionsHonest(list, {stillNeeded})` in `helpers/suite-files.ts` would make the four-part convention one call, and would mean the fifth guard gets the "still needed" half for free rather than by whoever writes it remembering.

- **`suiteFiles()` is called up to four times inside one `it` and re-walks the directory each time** — `readdirSync` plus a `statSync` per entry, times ~290 files, times every case that filters it. It is fast enough that nothing notices, and it is also the exact shape (`const files = suiteFiles()` hoisted in some cases, called inline in others) that makes a reader wonder whether the two calls could disagree. Memoising in the helper is safe — each suite is its own process and the tree does not change mid-run — and would make the answer obviously one answer.

- **The three readers are unmemoised and `suiteCode` is the expensive one** — every sweep reads and strips ~290 files, and `repo-file-helper.test.ts` alone now runs three sweeps. `stripComments` is a character-by-character scan. Same argument as above and slightly more of it; the counter-argument (a cache that can only ever hit within one process) is weaker here because a sweep hits every file and there are now six sweeps.

- **`topLevelSuites()` claims to be "the set `npm test` runs" and only one case checks that against `package.json`** — the glob lives in the test script and the helper's doc asserts the correspondence in prose. If the script ever grows a second glob or an `--exclude`, the helper keeps answering confidently and every rule built on it quietly narrows. The check belongs in the helper's own guard as a parse of the script rather than a `match` on it.

- **Nothing walks `scripts/` or `supabase/functions/` the way this now walks `__tests__/`** — several suites sweep those trees with hand-rolled recursive walks (`masonry-callers.test.ts`, `sort-helpers.test.ts`, `analytics-pii.test.ts`, `soft-destructive-chip.test.ts` and others each have their own `walk(dir)`), and they are the same function with different filters. That is a bigger and less obviously-shaped consolidation than this one — the filters genuinely differ — but it is the same argument at roughly the same scale the suite walk was at this morning.


### Root-rule follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **Four guards now walk `__tests__` with four copies of the same twelve-line `suiteFiles()`** — `repo-file-helper.test.ts`, `i18n-source-file.test.ts`, `edge-function-source-helper.test.ts` and `lint-guard-premise.test.ts` each declare the readdir-one-level-deep walk, and two of them also declare `flattened`/`code` (flatten whitespace, optionally strip comments first). Three of those four were written TODAY, by the runs that removed the same duplication one level down. A `__tests__/helpers/suite-files.ts` exporting `suiteFiles()`, `suiteText(rel)` and `suiteCode(rel)` is the obvious next consolidation, and the fourth copy is the argument for doing it now rather than at ten.

- **The `__dirname` sweep found forty-three suites the `process.cwd()` sweep had passed, and nothing says how the NEXT route would be found** — `import.meta.url`, `path.resolve(".")`, an env var, a `require.resolve` of a repo module. The guard names two derivations in a list, which is honest and is still a list of shapes. The rule that would not need extending is the inverse one: a suite may not compute an ABSOLUTE path at all except through the helper — matchable as "no `path.resolve`/`path.isAbsolute`/leading-slash literal outside `helpers/`". Worth a look before the third route arrives.

- **`repoPath("__tests__")` is now written in four suites and the suite directory is a fact like any other** — the same argument that gave `I18N_SOURCE_REL` its own constant. If the `suite-files.ts` helper above lands it takes this with it; if it does not, a `SUITES_REL` beside `REPO_ROOT` is two lines.

- **Two suites still slice paths by hand where `repoRelative` now exists** — `analytics-pii.test.ts` had three `file.slice(ROOT.length + 1)` (fixed in this pass), and the shape is easy to write again because it looks like string work rather than path work. It is also subtly wrong on a root with a trailing separator. A sweep for `.slice(` applied to something ending in `.length` inside the suite directory would catch the next one; whether that is worth a guard or just a line in the helper doc is the open question.

- **`guard-fixture.ts` spawns with `cwd: REPO_ROOT` and that is now the only bare use of the root left outside the helper** — it is real (a spawned guard has to be told where to run) and it is invisible to the rule, since the guard reads suites and this is a helper. Either the helper grows the one shape that needs it (`spawnAtRepoRoot`), or the exemption is stated where a reader of the rule will find it. Today it is stated nowhere.


### Root-sweep follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The root rule is stripped-of-comments and the layout rule is not** — `repo-file-helper.test.ts` runs its `process.cwd()` sweep over `stripComments(source)` so a doc comment can explain the shape it retired, and the new `edge-function-source-helper.test.ts` sweep does the same. But `i18n-source-file.test.ts`, the third guard in the family, still matches raw flattened text and therefore needs its exemption for prose reasons rather than code ones. One `suiteCode(relative)` in `__tests__/helpers` — the flatten-and-strip the three guards each declare — would make all three say the rule the same way, and would let the i18n one shrink its exemption list to the files that genuinely open the path.

- **`REPO_ROOT` is now exported for two reasons and only one of them is a caller** — `privacy-baseline-provenance-script.test.ts` passes it as a `cwd` to `spawnSync`, which is a real use the three helpers do not cover, and `repo-file-helper.test.ts` reads it to pin what it resolves to. Every other suite wants `repoPath`. A `spawnAtRepoRoot`-shaped helper (or just documenting that `cwd: REPO_ROOT` is the one sanctioned bare use) would close the last hole in "a suite does not name the root" without a fourth exemption.

- **Eight suites still build `supabase/functions/<name>/index.ts` from a literal name and the new sweep deliberately lets them** — `repoPath("supabase", "functions", "sign-upload", "index.ts")` in `sign-upload-function.test.ts` and seven siblings state one path once, which is what every other single-file suite does, so the guard only matches the layout being built from a VARIABLE. That is defensible and it is also eight more places that would need editing if the layout ever moves — the exact argument that carried `readI18nSource`. Worth deciding out loud: either they go through `readEdgeFunction("sign-upload")` too, or the guard's comment says why one-per-file is the line.

- **The migration was a codemod and nothing in the repo records that** — 91 files changed by a script that rewrote `path.join(process.cwd(), …)` shapes, renamed local root consts, and pruned the imports it orphaned. It renamed three words inside PROSE on the way (`must wrap the root component` became `must wrap the REPO_ROOT component`), caught by reading the diff rather than by any check. A future sweep of this size would be safer with the codemod committed under `scripts/` — or, cheaper, with a guard that fails on an ALL-CAPS helper name appearing inside a string literal or comment, which is the specific damage a blind identifier rename does.

- **`readRepoFile` and `repoPath` now take `(first: string, ...rest: readonly string[])` and that broke two call sites in ways worth generalising** — `CONSUMERS.map(readRepoFile)` stopped typechecking (map passes an index into `rest`) and `readRepoFile(...segments)` behind a local wrapper stopped too (a `string[]` spread has no tuple type). Both were improvements once fixed, and both were found by `tsc` rather than by design. If any other helper here grows a rest parameter, the same two shapes will break silently for anyone iterating — a line in the module doc naming them would cost nothing.


### Inline-read follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The two guards now check each other and neither says so** — the layering case in `repo-file-helper.test.ts` turned the i18n "said once" sweep red by naming the file inline, which is the guards working exactly as intended and is invisible to a reader of either. A line in each pointing at the other ("a case here that names a specific file will be caught there") would turn a confusing red into an expected one.

- **`process.cwd()` still appears about 120 times under `__tests__`, now almost entirely in directory walks** — `readdirSync(path.join(process.cwd(), dir))`, `walk(path.join(process.cwd(), root))`, and a long tail of `const ROOT = process.cwd()` kept for a non-read purpose. A `repoPath(...segments)` beside `readRepoFile` would finish the job, and would let the guard match ANY `process.cwd()` in a suite — the simplest rule available, and the only one that stays true as new shapes arrive.

- **`readRepoFile` takes a slash-joined string and the walks it would replace take segments** — the migration wrote `readRepoFile("supabase/functions/x/index.ts")`, which is fine on both platforms Node supports and is a different convention from the `path.join(a, b, c)` the rest of the suite directory uses. Worth deciding once: either the helper takes `...segments`, or the doc says slash-joined is the convention and why.

- **`assert-caller.test.ts` and `cors-shared.test.ts` had the same `fnSource(name)` function, and they still do** — the migration made both one line instead of four and left two copies of "the source of a supabase function, by name". Two is not a hundred and fourteen; it is also exactly how the hundred and fourteen started.

- **The inline-literal sweep matches `readFileSync( path.join( process.cwd(), "` and nothing else** — a suite that writes `fs.readFileSync`, or assigns `const cwd = process.cwd()` first, walks straight past it. That is the same near-miss the `.length, 6` guard had this morning with a literal space. The rule the previous entry proposes (any `process.cwd()` at all) is the one that does not have a next near-miss.


### Repo-file follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **A hundred and fifty suites now import the helper under a local alias and the alias is `read` in nearly all of them** — `import { readRepoFile as read }` kept the migration to one line per file, which was the right trade for a 154-file change and leaves the call sites saying `read("lib/foo.ts")`, a name that says nothing about what it reads or where from. A follow-up pass renaming the call sites to `readRepoFile` would finish the thought; it is a large diff with no behaviour in it, which is exactly the kind of change to do on its own or not at all.

- **About a hundred suites still open a specific file inline and the guard deliberately ignores them** — `readFileSync(path.join(process.cwd(), "app", "index.tsx"), "utf8")` at the top of a suite is not the duplication this removed, and it IS the same root spelled again. Routing those through `readRepoFile("app/index.tsx")` would remove the last places a suite says how to reach the tree; the guard would then be able to match any `process.cwd()` in a suite at all, which is a much simpler rule than the four shapes it enumerates today.

- **The floor is `readers.length >= 120` and the number will drift down as suites are deleted** — it is set well under today's ~150 and, unlike the lint guards' floors, nothing re-measures it. Same standing question as the `i18n-source-file` floors filed this morning: either these belong in the lint-guard floor-raising habit, or the comment should say they are deliberately un-maintained.

- **`REPO_ROOT` is exported and read by one suite** — the helper offers a root and a reader, and every real caller wants the reader. The export exists because the guard asserts what the root resolves to. Same shape as `I18N_SOURCE_PATH`, and now that there are two of them it is a pattern rather than a one-off: a `__tests__/helpers` convention for "exported for the guard that pins it" would be worth stating once.

- **Three helper modules now sit in `__tests__/helpers` with overlapping jobs** — `repo-file.ts` (any file), `i18n-source-file.ts` (one file), `i18n-locales.ts` (what that file declares). `readI18nSource` could now be one line of `readRepoFile`, which would make the layering visible instead of leaving two independent readers of the same tree. Small, and it is the kind of small that stops being obvious in a month.


### Helper-naming follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **155 suites still define `read(rel)` for themselves and the i18n consolidation only took the i18n line out of them** — `readFileSync(path.join(process.cwd(), rel), "utf8")` is written out in about 90 files, and a further 65 spell a `ROOT`/`REPO_ROOT`/`repoRoot` const first. Two of them differ in ways nobody chose (`dev-globals-declaration.test.ts` passes the path through unjoined; `bundle-smoke.test.ts` goes through `fs.`), which is what 155 copies of a two-line function eventually produces. A `readRepoFile(rel)` beside `readI18nSource` is the same argument that carried the i18n one, at fifty times the scale — worth doing as its own pass, and worth doing with a guard so the 156th is not written.

- **`abbreviate` is 72 characters chosen by eye** — it collapses whitespace and cuts, which is right, and the number is a guess about terminal width in a repo that has no other opinion about message length. Several assertions elsewhere print whole file slices. Either the constant belongs somewhere shared with a sentence saying what it is for, or the helper should take the limit and let the two or three callers that care differ.

- **The `Body` suffix says what is matched and not that it is the weak form** — `assertMatchesInEveryLocaleBody` reads as a neutral description, and the module doc is what carries "reach for this last". A reader who skims the exports and picks by name still picks it. If it stays, the doc line above the function itself (rather than only the module block) should open with the warning; if the call sites ever drop to one or two, it should stop being exported.

- **Nothing checks that the two Body helpers are actually rare** — the ordering in the module doc is advice, and advice about test style is what the lint guards exist to enforce elsewhere in this repo. A floor-style guard ("at most N suites import a Body helper", with N at today's count) would make a new weak-form call site a visible decision rather than the path of least resistance.

- **`localeStrings` now accepts single quotes and no fixture in the real tree has any** — the alternation is exercised only by a synthetic fixture, so if the regex is ever rewritten the single-quote branch can rot without anything noticing. That is acceptable for a two-branch regex and worth naming: it is a capability the repo does not use, added because the alternative was an undocumented dependency on prettier's config.


### Value-span follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The paren counter fixed value spans and quietly changed what `atTopLevel()` means for KEYS too** — a key written inside unbalanced parens is now invisible where before it was found. No such key exists in the file and none could type-check against `TranslationMap`, so nothing moved; the point is that the change was made for the span collector and lands on every reader of the scan. A case asserting the key set of `lib/i18n-context.tsx` is byte-identical before and after would have said so out loud — the suite proved it incidentally by staying green, which is not the same as recording it.

- **`values` is a third structure derived from one walk and nothing pins it against the other two** — `keys`, `spreads` and `values` come out of the same scan, and the only assertion tying them together is per-locale (`values.size === keys.length`). The interesting agreement is the one that can break: a key in `keys` with no entry in `values` is a scan that stopped early, and the parser could state that as an invariant at the point of return rather than leaving it for a suite to check on the real file.

- **`localeStrings` refuses anything but a double-quoted literal, and the file has single quotes nowhere by accident** — prettier normalises them, so `/^"([^"]*)"$/` holds today because of a formatter setting, not because of the language. The same reasoning that moved key-finding off the two-space indent applies: either the helper should unwrap whichever quote it finds, or its doc should say it depends on prettier and would need a change the day the config does.

- **`assertValueInEveryLocale` prints the whole rejected value and some of them are long** — the failure message is a real improvement over "not matched in de" and it inlines a template that can run past a terminal line, several locales at a time. Truncating to the first ~60 characters with an ellipsis, or printing one locale per line, would keep the gain without the wall.

- **Eight suites now import between one and five helpers from `i18n-locales` and the choice between them is a naming question** — `assertDeclaredInEveryLocale`, `assertMatchesInEveryLocale`, `assertMatchesInEveryNonBaseLocale`, `assertValueInEveryLocale`, `localeValuesOf`, `localeStrings`, `localeBodies`. Every one has a reason to exist and the set is now large enough that picking wrong is easy — `assertMatchesInEveryLocale` in particular is the one that should almost never be reached for, and its name is the most general. Worth either a doc block at the top of the module ordering them by "reach for this first", or renaming the body-matching pair to say `InEveryLocaleBody`.


### Slice-retirement follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The guard was wrong about whitespace once and there is no reason to think it is the last time** — it matched `.length, 6` with a literal space and missed prettier's wrapped form, which is how three suites stayed green while still counting. The fix widened one regex; the class of bug is "a guard written against source TEXT is a guard against one formatting of it". The repo already owns a parser for the one file where this mattered most. A guard that read the suites through a real parse — even a crude one that finds `assert.equal` call sites and looks at their arguments — would be immune to the next wrapping, and there are now four text-scanning guards under `__tests__` that share the exposure.

- **`assertMatchesInEveryNonBaseLocale` and `assertMatchesInEveryLocale` differ by one `continue`** — and by which of them a call site should use, which is a judgement no type enforces: passing a base-inclusive pattern to the non-base helper silently checks five locales instead of six. A single `assertMatches(source, pattern, label, { include: "all" | "inheriting" })` would put the choice in the call and make it readable at the call site, rather than encoding it in a name that is one word longer.

- **Every migrated "override" case is now equivalent to its sibling "declares" case, and both still run** — `assertDeclaredInEveryLocale(src, key)` says every locale declares the key; `assertMatchesInEveryNonBaseLocale(src, /key: "[^"]+"/, …)` says every inheriting locale declares it as a non-empty string. The second implies most of the first, and six suites now run both against the same key under two `it()` names inherited from when they meant different (broken) things. Worth one pass to decide which claim each file actually wants.

- **Nothing checks that a fixture built to fool the old rule still fools it** — the strength of the new cases rests on `assert.match(RU_MISSING_ES_HAS, slice)`, i.e. on the old pattern being written out correctly in the test. If someone simplifies that literal while tidying, the case keeps passing and stops demonstrating anything. Naming the retired patterns in one exported const — read by both the demonstration and by a guard asserting no suite still uses them — would tie the two together.

- **`localeBodies` is called once per locale per assertion and re-parses the file each time** — `assertMatchesInEveryNonBaseLocale` calls it once, but a loop over four keys calls it four times, and each call runs `findLocaleBlock` six times over a 200KB source. Irrelevant at the current suite size (the whole run is unchanged), and it is the kind of thing that becomes visible at 30 keys. A per-source memo inside the helper module would be honest here in a way it was not for the file read, since these calls are all in one process.


### Per-locale-assertion follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The `localizes X in ru / be / pl / de / es` cases still slice the file with `const ${lang}: TranslationMap = \{[\s\S]*?\};`** — five suites keep that shape beside the assertions just migrated, and it is the "slice to the next `const`" habit the parser was written to replace: it depends on the annotation (`en` does not carry one, so the pattern silently never matches the base map) and on `};` appearing nowhere inside a value. `localeBodies` already returns exactly those bodies. Migrating them finishes the job on the same eleven files rather than leaving two idioms side by side.

- **`assertMatchesInEveryLocale` takes a pattern that can stride past its own key** — the doc says so and nothing enforces it: `/loadMoreItems:[\s\S]*?params\?\.count \?\? 0/` matched against one map still matches if `loadMoreItems` lacks the fallback and some LATER key in the same map has it. Scoping to a body shrank the window; it did not close it. A `valueOf(source, code, key)` returning just that key's declared value — which the scanner could offer, since it already walks to the key — would make these assertions exact instead of merely local.

- **Eleven suites now import three helpers each and none of them asks for a whole key at once** — the migrated call sites read `assertDeclaredInEveryLocale(src, key)` followed by `assertMatchesInEveryLocale(src, /key: "[^"]+"/, …)`, which is two statements of one intent ("every locale declares this key, as a non-empty string"). An `assertStringKeyInEveryLocale(src, key)` covering the common case would collapse most of the twenty-two into one line each and leave the pattern form for the genuinely shape-specific ones.

- **The sweep guard keys on `readI18nSource` and the habit is not limited to that file** — `__tests__/i18n-locales-helper.test.ts` flags a suite only if it reads the translations source AND counts to six. The same "count the hits, assert N" shape is how several suites check `components/item-filters.tsx` and `app/collection/[id].tsx` too, and nothing watches those. Either the guard should widen to any `.match(/…/g)` whose length is compared to a literal, or its comment should say it is deliberately scoped to the locale case.

- **`bundle-smoke.test.ts` is exempted by filename, which is the weakest form of the exemption** — the reason is written down (its six counts privacy pages, not locales) and the mechanism is a string match on the file's name, so a rename silently drops the exemption and a genuinely offending case added to that file is never seen. Anchoring the exemption on the counted expression (`PRIVACY_PAGE_TARGETS.length`) rather than on the file would exempt the fact instead of the location.


### One-path follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **Fifty suites read the source and most of them still match it with a regex** — the path is one fact now and what the suites do with the text is not: `sort-options-parity.test.ts` counts `^  labelKey: "…",$` occurrences and asserts six, `reorder-blocked-notice.test.ts` does the same for two keys, and a dozen others test `\bkey:` against the whole file. Every one of those is asking "does each locale declare this key", which `localeKeys(source, code)` answers without depending on prettier's indentation — and the six-hits count in particular reports a key declared twice in one locale and missing from another as green. The parser exists; the callers that predate it have not moved.

- **`read(rel)` survives in thirty-odd suites and is now a helper with one caller each** — the migration took the i18n line out of those files and left the local `read(rel)` behind for the one or two other files each suite opens. Thirty definitions of `readFileSync(path.join(process.cwd(), rel), "utf8")` is the same duplication one level up, and the same rename argument applies to `app/collection/[id].tsx` and `components/item-filters.tsx`. A single `readRepoFile(rel)` in `__tests__/helpers/` would finish the job the i18n helper started.

- **The exemption list is asserted and the reason each entry is on it is not** — `ALLOWED_TO_SPELL_THE_PATH` is pinned to exactly two files, which stops it growing quietly; what the case cannot say is WHY each is there. If the pin were `[HELPER, THIS_SUITE]` with the second derived from the file's own name rather than typed as a literal, a rename of the suite would move the exemption with it instead of silently exempting a file that no longer exists — which the staleness case catches, but only after someone has been confused by it.

- **The floors are two numbers chosen by hand in a repo that measures its floors** — `> 200` walked files and `>= 40` readers were picked to sit well under today's counts, the same way the lint guards' floors are, and unlike those they are not re-measured anywhere. The lint-guard suite has a documented habit of raising floors when a scan root grows; these two are outside it, so they will drift downward in usefulness rather than go red. Worth either folding into that habit or saying in the comment that they are deliberately un-maintained.

- **`I18N_SOURCE_PATH` is exported and only the guard suite reads it** — the helper offers a path and a reader, and every real caller wants the reader. The path is public because the case asserting the resolution needs it, which is a test reaching into a module's internals through its public surface. Either the assertion should go through `readI18nSource()` alone (comparing against a `readFileSync` the suite spells itself, which it already does) or the export should carry a doc line saying its only reader is a test.


### Numeric-literal follow-ups (2026-08-18)

- **The phantom-key bug says the scanner should decline numeric keys OUT LOUD, and it still declines them by silence** — `0x2f:` used to become a key named `x2f`; it now becomes nothing at all, which is correct and is indistinguishable from the literal not having that entry. The module's doc lists quoted and computed keys as deliberate omissions because both are compile errors against `TranslationMap`; a numeric key is the third member of that family and is not mentioned. Adding it to the list costs a line, and the case that pins the fix would then be pinning a documented stance rather than an implementation detail.

- **`NUMERIC_LITERAL` is permissive by design and nothing states the boundary it must not cross** — `1__0` and `1_` both match, deliberately, because a malformed literal is a compile error long before it reaches the scanner. But permissiveness has a limit that IS load-bearing: the pattern must never consume a character that begins the next token, or the scan resyncs one token late and the `/` rule reads the wrong `prev`. `1..toString()` is the shape that tests it (the first `.` belongs to the number, the second to the member access), and no case covers it. Worth one fixture rather than one more sentence of doc.

- **The three separator cases could be two if the strict pattern were derived once** — `shows, for every row…` builds `" +"` or `"\\n"` inline and the mutation case rebuilds the inline half a second time, so the strictness that the field exists to restore is written twice and can drift on one side. A tiny `rowPattern(word, separator)` helper beside the table, exported or not, would make the mutation case assert against the same regex the row check uses — which is the only way it genuinely proves what it claims.

- **`separator` describes the example and is read as describing the WORD** — the field's values are facts about a string in the table, but every case treats them as facts about the language (`"newline"` ⇒ the word ends a statement). Those coincide today because the newline set is asserted to be exactly the three statement-enders. If a fourth ASI row ever lands, the assertion listing the three by name is the thing that breaks, and the fix will be to widen the list rather than to notice that the field never meant what it was being read as. Naming the property in the type as "what the grammar permits" rather than "how the example is written" would keep the two from separating.

- **The unassertable-claims block names three and there is no rule for a fourth** — the note beside `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` is a good record and is also a list that grows by hand, which is the recall problem the keyword table escaped. There is no test that fails when a fourth such claim is added and not listed, and there cannot be one. What could exist is a convention — a `@grammar-claim` marker in the doc comment of each, and a case counting the markers against the block's own count — so the block goes stale loudly instead of quietly.


### Reserved-word-partition follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **The partition is three structures in two files and only one of them is in `lib/`** — `REGEXP_FOLLOWS_KEYWORD` lives in `lib/i18n-source.ts`, while `RESERVED_WORDS` and `DIVIDES_AFTER` (the other half of the same partition, and the authority it is checked against) live in `__tests__/i18n-source.test.ts`. That is the right split while the audit is a test, but it means the module ships half a partition: a reader of the table cannot see what was deliberately excluded without opening a suite. If the standing suggestion to move the keyword rule to a `lib/js-scan.ts` is ever taken, the exclusion record should travel with it as the module's own statement of scope, leaving the suite with the assertions rather than the data.

- **`CONTEXTUAL_KEYWORDS` is eight words chosen by judgement, which is the recall problem one level down** — `RESERVED_WORDS` is a spec production and is exhaustive by construction; the contextual list is not derived from anything, and it has to exist because `of` is a table row and is not a reserved word. So the exhaustiveness case proves the two sides cover `RESERVED_WORDS ∪ CONTEXTUAL_KEYWORDS` and says nothing about whether that union is the language. Listing which productions were consulted (`for`-`of`, accessor property names, `import`/`export` clauses, class element modifiers) in the doc comment would at least make an omission reviewable instead of invisible — the same argument that made the reserved half exhaustive, applied to the half that cannot be.

- **The row-shape check now accepts any whitespace between the word and its regex, so a row can claim more than it has** — `break`, `continue` and `debugger` put their `/re/` on the NEXT LINE, which is why the assertion moved from `includes("word /")` to `\bword\s*/re/`. That loosening applies to every row: a `return` row rewritten with a line break would still pass, and the distinction that matters to the scanner (can the regex stand on the SAME line, i.e. is there a division to get wrong) is now unstated. A second field per row — the separator the example uses, or a boolean — would keep the two claims apart and would let the same-line rows be checked more strictly than they are.

- **The `.` in `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` rests on an argument nothing executable can check** — the trailing-dot fix is justified by "a `.` cannot be the token before a `/` in any valid program", which is true and is a claim about the GRAMMAR, not about this scanner. It cannot be turned into a case: the counter-example (`p./re/`) is a syntax error, so it cannot be written in a fixture that compiles, and the scanner would happily read it either way. The doc comment is the only place the argument lives. Worth naming as a class of claim this file now has three of (this one, the ASI rows, and the newline bound on `skipRegExpLiteral`) — each load-bearing, each unassertable.

- **`consumed()` is documented as one call per token and the loop tail calls it per CHARACTER** — the identifier and punctuator branches record whole tokens, but the fallthrough at the bottom (`if (!/\s/.test(ch)) consumed(ch)`) fires for every other non-whitespace character, so a number like `1_000n` is six calls, an operator like `===` is three. Harmless, because only the last character survives and the word is cleared either way — and it is why the scanner has no numeric-literal branch at all and still divides correctly after every numeric form. But "one call per token" is now the invariant a reader is asked to hold while the busiest call site does not obey it; either the tail deserves a note or the numeric forms deserve a branch that makes the claim true.


### Keyword-table follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **The table is derived and still lives beside the one caller that reads it** — `REGEXP_FOLLOWS_KEYWORD` and `REGEXP_KEYWORDS` (its key-set, rebuilt at module load) sit in `lib/i18n-source.ts`, a file whose whole job is one repository's translation shapes. The scanner is now general enough that the keyword rule is the reusable part and the locale-block readers are the specific part; if a second file is ever scanned, the table wants to move to a `lib/js-scan.ts` beside `scanLiteral` rather than travel under an i18n name. Not worth doing for one caller — worth naming so the next scanner does not copy the list a third time.

### Member-access follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **The scanner tracks `prev` and `prevWord` and a third reader would want the token KIND** — every rule that reads back is reconstructing "what kind of token was that" from its last character and its word, and the member-dot fix adds a third reconstruction (is this identifier a property name). A small token-kind enum recorded by `consumed()` — `identifier` / `keyword-eligible` / `punctuator` / `string` — would let the `/` rule ask `prevKind === "keyword" && REGEXP_KEYWORDS.has(prevWord)` directly, and would make the next lookbehind rule a field read rather than a fourth string heuristic. Worth it only when a third such rule appears; noting it so that rule does not add a fourth `prev*` variable instead.

### Token-recorder follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **A misread `/` costs one line and nothing says so where it matters** — `skipRegExpLiteral` stops at a newline, which is why every stale-word mutation resyncs and why no assertion can catch one. That bound is the reason three of this file's hazards are survivable, and it is written in the helper's own doc comment where a reader of the `/` rule will not look. Naming it beside `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` would turn "we cannot test that" into "here is why it is bounded".

- **`consumed()` is called once per token and the identifier branch calls it twice** — the key path records the identifier and then records the `:` that followed it, which is correct and is also the one place where the invariant "one call per token" reads as two. A comment, or recording once after the lookahead resolves, would keep the shape self-evident.

- **`scanObjectBody` and `scanArrayBody` are thin wrappers whose only job is to narrow a type** — worth it (an object scan can no longer be asked for element spans), and it means `scanLiteral` now has no direct caller outside them. Whether the closer parameter should still exist, or whether the two wrappers should each own their loop, is the question a third literal shape would answer.

- **Three tables of six language codes are checked against the picker and against nothing else** — also left standing: `EXPECTED_LANGUAGES`, `TRANSLATION_LANGUAGES` and `PRIVACY_PAGE_LANGUAGES`. A seventh language means three edits to make three suites green, which is the moment to decide whether the parity net wants one table with three readers.

### Keyword-rule follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **`REGEXP_FOLLOWS_KEYWORD` has fourteen entries and one is exercised** — `return` is the row a case covers; the other thirteen were written from memory of where a regex may follow a word. `of` and `in` are the interesting ones (both appear in `for (const x of …)`, neither can precede a regex there) and `instanceof` cannot precede one at all. A case per keyword is thirteen lines of fixture for a file that holds none of them; naming the source — ECMA-262's `RegularExpressionLiteral` lookahead restrictions — would at least let the next reader check the list against something.

- **The scan now carries two variables that must be cleared together and are cleared in eleven places** — `prev` and `prevWord` are set in every branch, and a new branch that forgets `prevWord = ""` leaves a stale keyword one token too long: `return "x" / 2` would open a regex. That is one added branch away, and the fix that removes the hazard rather than documenting it is a single `advance(ch, word)` helper the branches call instead of assigning both by hand.

- **`prevWord` is cleared on whitespace-only for the character rule and kept for the word rule, and nothing pins that difference** — `prev` deliberately survives whitespace (`n / 2` divides) and so does `prevWord` (`return  /x/` opens a regex, two spaces and all). Both are right and both are accidents of one `if (!/\s/.test(ch))`. A case with a newline between the keyword and the slash — real prettier output for a long ternary — would pin the half that is easiest to lose.

- **`collectElements` makes the scan's return type lie for object literals** — `ScanResult.elements` is `readonly string[]` for both closers and is now always empty for `}`, so a future caller reading `elements` off an object scan gets a silent empty list rather than a type error. Splitting the result (or making `elements` optional and undefined for objects) would say which scans can answer.

- **The four suites that read `languageOptions` now agree, and three of them still hard-code the six codes beside it** — `EXPECTED_LANGUAGES`, `TRANSLATION_LANGUAGES` and `PRIVACY_PAGE_LANGUAGES` are three copies of the same list, each checked against the picker in its own suite. That is the parity net working, and it means adding a seventh language means editing three tables to make three suites go green — the point at which a run stops asking whether the tables should be one.

### One-parser follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **`localeKeys` throws and `findLocaleBlock` returns null, and the same suites now use both** — the throw is right for a caller asserting parity (an empty set reports a renamed block as 538 missing translations), and the null is right for a caller asking whether a language exists at all. Sixteen suites now pick between them by which import someone copied, and the one that picks wrong turns a deleted map into either an unreadable stack or a green vacuous pass. Naming the distinction in the module doc — or making the null form `findLocaleBlockOrNull` — would make the choice deliberate rather than incidental.

- **The scanner's regex branch rests on a one-character lookbehind and nothing pins the case it gets wrong** — `DIVISION_FOLLOWS` reads the last significant character, which is the standard heuristic and is wrong after a keyword: `return /x/` and `typeof /x/` both put an identifier character before a `/` that opens a regex. No translation value can contain either today (values are strings and single-expression arrows), so the gap is theoretical in this file and would arrive the moment the scanner is pointed at a second one. A case asserting the known-wrong input — with the doc line saying so — is cheaper than the keyword set and keeps the limitation from being discovered by a wrong count.

- **Sixteen suites spell the path to `lib/i18n-context.tsx` for themselves** — one parser now, and sixteen `path.join(process.cwd(), "lib", "i18n-context.tsx")`s in front of it, four of them wrapped in a local `read()` helper. A `readI18nSource()` beside the parser would make the path a fact in one place. [corrected 2026-08-17: the first draft of this entry offered memoisation as the win, which is wrong — `tsx --test` runs each suite in its own process, so the sixteen reads happen either way and a cache would only ever hit within one file. The path spelling is the whole benefit, which makes this a naming change and not a performance one.]

- **`elements` is collected on every scan and read by exactly one caller** — `scanLiteral` records the top-level object spans whether it was started on an array or an object, so every locale-map scan builds a list of ~40 nested-value spans that nothing reads. Harmless at this size and a real allocation per call, and the honest fix is either a flag or a note saying the array reader is why it exists.

- **The picker's label parity is asserted against `PRIVACY_PAGE_LANGUAGES` and its ORDER against `TRANSLATION_LANGUAGES`, in two suites that do not know about each other** — `findLanguageOptions` returns rows in picker order and `__tests__/i18n-source.test.ts` pins that order, while `privacy-page-i18n.test.ts` sorts before comparing. A reordered picker (a plausible product change: coverage-sorted, as the still-open first entry of "Translation-coverage follow-ups" proposes) turns one suite red and leaves the other silent, which is the wrong way round — the order is a design decision and the label parity is the invariant.

### Translation-coverage follow-ups (2026-08-17)

- **The number is measured and nothing acts on it** — four locales sit below half and the suite prints it, which is a report rather than a policy. The cheap next step is deciding what the picker owes a 38%-translated language: offering `de` beside `ru` implies parity that does not exist, and the alternatives are all one-liners (badge the incomplete ones, sort by coverage, hide below a threshold) sitting on a number that now exists. Worth answering before the next feature widens the gap by another twenty keys.

- **`TRANSLATION_FLOORS` catches a locale losing translations and nothing catches a locale gaining an untranslated key** — the reasoning for that is written down (a ceiling on inherited keys goes red on every feature PR), and it leaves the ordinary case unmeasured: a run that adds fifteen English strings and translates none of them moves five rows and trips nothing. A DELTA published in the commit — "en +15, ru +15, be +0" — would make the widening visible at review time without gating anything, and the coverage rows already carry both halves.

- **`declaredKeysByIndentation` is exported for one comparison and reads like a utility** — it is the second statement of a rule, and the case that uses it is the only reason it is public. A future reader looking for "the keys of this literal" will find two exported functions, pick the shorter one, and get a reader that is wrong about multi-line templates. Naming it for its role (`declaredKeysByFormatting`, or a doc line saying it exists to disagree) would stop the comparison being quietly repurposed into a parser.

- **The scanner handles regex literals nowhere and the translations contain none** — `/` is treated as a comment opener when doubled and as an ordinary character otherwise, which is correct for this file and wrong for JavaScript in general. Nothing in `lib/i18n-context.tsx` will ever hold a regex value, so the gap is not a bug today; it becomes one the moment this parser is pointed at a second file, which is exactly what a general-sounding name like `parseObjectLiteral` invites. Either the doc comment says "this file's shapes only", or the scanner learns the one branch.

- **Three suites now parse `lib/i18n-context.tsx` and each locates its blocks differently** — `i18n-translations.test.ts` matches `const ru = {` by regex, `i18n-locale-map-parity.test.ts` scrapes `languageOptions`, and this run's module scans for the matching brace. Three readers of one file, agreeing today because the file is formatted the way all three expect. `findLocaleBlock` is the one that does not depend on formatting, so the other two could read through it and the file would have a single parser rather than three habits.

### Danger-icon-button follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **Two destructive surfaces one channel apart is a palette question nobody has answered** — `DESTRUCTIVE_ICON_SURFACE` and `SOFT_DESTRUCTIVE_SURFACE` differ on all three slots (`CARD_BG_8`/`CARD_BG_10`, `DANGER_SOFT`/`DANGER_SOFT_2`, `DANGER_DEEP_3`/`DANGER_DEEP_4`) and the difference is invisible at arm's length. The extraction deliberately did not merge them, because repainting a shipped row is a design decision and not a refactor — but the decision is now written down in exactly one place and can be made cheaply: repoint `DESTRUCTIVE_ICON_SURFACE` at the soft trio, look at the wishlist row, keep or revert. If it merges, six tokens collapse to three and the "one channel off" drift the family keeps warning about loses its last live example.

- **`<DangerIconButton>` has one caller and the entry that asked for it named three more** — item card, collection card and listing card each render per-row actions without a delete affordance today, so the widget's second adopter is a feature and not a migration. Worth pinning which of the three is actually wanted before the button grows a `size` prop for a denser row: a widget with one caller and three speculative ones is where optional props come from.

- **`app/collection/[id].tsx` is still the lone holdout importing `CARD_BG_10` + `DANGER_SOFT_2` directly** — flagged by `soft-destructive-chip.test.ts` a run ago and unchanged: its delete button is the block form at `RADIUS_CARD` rather than the pill both widgets use, so adopting `<DangerSection>` would round its corners. Now that the family has three members, the cheapest resolution may be a fourth — a `tone`/`radius` seam on `<DangerSection>` — rather than leaving one screen permanently outside the table.

- **The `deleteItem` key existed in three of six languages and nobody noticed for as long as it was unused** — `pl`/`de`/`es` inherit `...en`, so a missing translation renders English and passes every test. This run added the three, but only because a new a11y label reached for the key; the family around it (`deleteItemTitle`, `deleteItemText`) is still English in those three locales. A test that counts per-language keys NOT overridden from `en` would turn a silent fallback into a number that can be watched, and the number is the honest one to publish rather than "six languages supported".

- **Two lint-guard floors are a component file away from each other and had to move together** — `check-inline-radius` and `check-analytics-imports` both sit one above `components/`'s own count, so every single component added to the repo will eventually draw level and redden `lint-guard-partial-root.test.ts` again. That is the floor working as designed, but the re-measure is manual and the failure arrives in a suite that has nothing to do with the change. A `npm run remeasure-floors` script that prints the current counts alongside each declared minimum would turn a five-minute archaeology dig into a diff.


### Path-namespace follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **The UNC hang was found by asserting a premise, and the other premise in this file has never been asserted the same way** — `looksLikePackageEntry` rests on "every specifier node resolves from here is a plain name or a `@scope`, never a leading dot", which is a claim about node's resolver stated in prose and checked only against entries someone thought of. The chain premise turned out false on its third platform after two runs of confident doc comments. Reading node's own `_nodeModulePaths` and resolver source into a case — or at least listing what npm/pnpm/yarn actually write into a `node_modules` — is the same exercise on the claim that has not had it.

- **`nearestChainLink` and `nodeModulesChain` take a path namespace and every other path operation in this file uses the ambient `path`** — `isUnder`, the chain-link probes and the refusal messages all join and compare with the runner's platform, so the win32 rows prove the two derivations agree and prove nothing about what happens to their answers afterwards. That is honest as far as it goes and the seam now stops one function short of being useful: a `resolvedFromCheckout` that took the same namespace would let the whole provenance walk be exercised against Windows paths without a Windows runner.

- **The child-process case pays ~1s of `node --import tsx` boot to prove a loop terminates** — worth it, and it is the second spawn in this file whose real job is a timeout rather than an environment. A shared `runInChild(script, { timeout })` helper beside `runGuardFrom` would make the third one cheap to write, and would put the "a synchronous loop cannot be interrupted by `node:test`'s timeout" reasoning in one place rather than in a comment on each case that needs it.

- **`isNodeModulesDir` is exported, has three cases, and both its callers pass it a namespace the cases mostly do not** — the near-miss rows (`my-node_modules`, `node_modules-old`) run on the ambient `path` only, and the separator handling is exactly what differs between namespaces: `path.win32.basename("a/node_modules")` and `path.posix.basename("a\\node_modules")` disagree. Running the predicate's rows against both would cost nothing and would cover the one thing the predicate actually does.

- **The suite now asserts facts about `path.win32` that are really assertions about the installed Node** — `dirname` of a UNC root returning itself, and `basename` returning the share, are behaviours of the runtime's `path` implementation, not of a spec. They are the right things to pin (a change there breaks this fixture) and the failure message would read as though the fixture were wrong. Wording those assertions as "this Node's `path.win32` behaves as the climb assumes" would send the next reader to the changelog rather than to the climb.

### Second-statement follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **The two derivations of "nearest" agree on every root the case tries and share the one assumption that could make both wrong** — `nearestChainLink` climbs out of a `node_modules` and `nodeModulesChain` skips one, and both spell the test as `path.basename(dir) === "node_modules"`. A checkout under a directory genuinely NAMED `node_modules` that is not one (rare, and pnpm's store layouts get close) is skipped by both, so the comparison stays green while both answers are wrong together. Nothing here can distinguish them; what would help is naming the shared test — `isNodeModulesDir(dir)` — so the assumption has one home rather than two spellings that look independent.

- **The platform premise is pinned for the platform the suite happens to run on** — `path.basename(FS_ROOT)` is `""` on the CI container's Linux, and the claim in both doc comments is about "every platform node runs on": Windows drive roots (`C:\`), UNC roots (`\\server\share\`) and POSIX all have to hold. `path.win32` and `path.posix` are importable and need no filesystem, so the case could assert the premise on both namespaces instead of on whichever one the runner booted with.

- **`nearestChainLink` no longer builds a chain and the two remaining callers of `nodeModulesChain` are the walk and the case that compares them** — which means the exported chain now exists mostly to be checked against. That is a legitimate role and it is worth writing down, because the next reader will see an export with one production caller and be tempted to inline it, taking the second statement of the rule with it.

- **`probeNearestLink` calls `nearestChainLink` and `probeChainLink` and the walk calls `nodeModulesChain` and `probeChainLink`, so the two ends of the clause now share no code at all** — that is the independence the comparison wanted for the PATH and it is not what anyone wants for the PROBE: a `ProbedLink` built two ways can differ in which path it keeps (asked-about versus resolved) with no case comparing the two. One constructor taking a path, or a case that runs both against a single planted root and deep-equals the results, would close the half that should not be independent.

- **Three cases now assert against `path.join("/a", "node_modules")` and friends, which are absolute POSIX paths written as literals in a suite that also runs `path.win32`-relevant claims** — they work because `path.join` normalises the separators and `/a` is a valid Windows-relative-to-current-drive path, which is a coincidence rather than a plan. Deriving the fake roots from `FS_ROOT` (`path.join(FS_ROOT, "a")`) would make them roots on every platform instead of on this one.

### Trailing-rest follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **`ChainLinks` guarantees the LAST entry and every reader uses the FIRST** — `readonly [...string[], string]` is the honest shape of the construction argument and it defends the end nobody reads: a walk that stopped pushing the nearest link, or one whose loop never ran, would still satisfy it. The compiler now catches a dropped root link and would not catch a dropped `below.push`. A type that guaranteed both ends (`readonly [string, ...string[], string]` for chains of two or more, with the one-link root case named separately) or a case asserting the first entry is the resolved root's own link would cover the half the readers actually depend on.

- **The loop now stops one directory short of the filesystem root, so "is this dir a `node_modules`" is never asked about that root** — it is skipped on the grounds that its basename is `""`, which is the argument for appending unconditionally, and it means the two halves of the walk apply different rules to different directories. Correct on every platform node runs on and worth a case that pins the premise rather than the conclusion: `path.basename(path.parse(cwd).root)` is `""`, on Windows drive roots and UNC roots too.

- **`nodeModulesChain` allocates a fresh list on every call and five of its readers want one entry** — `nearestChainLink`, `probeNearestLink` and the refusal paths each walk from a scratch root to the filesystem root and discard all but the first link. The standing memoisation suggestion covers the guard fleet's thirteen walks; this is the cheaper half of it, since a `nearestChainLink` defined directly (`join(resolve(root), "node_modules")` for a root that is not itself one) needs no list at all and would double as a second statement of the rule to check the chain's first entry against.

- **The no-duplicates property is now pinned and the walk's reuse is indexed, so nothing depends on it any more** — the case earns its place as documentation of why `index === 0` is safe, and it is worth noticing that it defends a property with no remaining reader. If a de-dup pass ever does land, the case will pass and the thing it was protecting will already have moved. Naming what depends on it in the case's own comment — or asserting the chain is strictly ancestor-ordered, which implies distinctness and is what the readers actually use — would keep it tied to something.

- **Three `describe` blocks now build `path.parse(path.resolve("/")).root` for themselves** — the filesystem root is the fixture that needs no plant and it is computed once per block, with `rootLink` derived in one of them and re-derived inline in the others. A shared constant at the top of the file (beside `REPO_ROOT`) would stop the fourth block inventing a fourth spelling, and would give the platform premise above one place to be asserted.

### Chain-invariant follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **The non-empty tuple is enforced by a throw whose branch nothing can reach, which is the shape just deleted three times over** — `nodeModulesChain` destructures its own list and refuses an empty one with a sentence no reader will ever see, on the grounds that a cast would let a future edit hand every caller an undefined nearest link. That reasoning is sound and the result is still an untestable branch in a file whose whole argument is that untestable branches rot. Building the chain so the root's link is appended LAST — the ancestors collected on the way up, the filesystem root's link added after the loop — would make non-emptiness structural, with no branch and no cast.

- **`nearestChainLink` walks the entire chain to the filesystem root and returns its first entry** — the nearest link is `path.join(path.resolve(root), "node_modules")` for every root that is not itself a `node_modules`, and the walk that computes the other N-1 entries is thrown away. It costs nothing today (one caller, on a refusal path) and it is the kind of thing that gets called from a loop later. More to the point, a direct definition would be a SECOND statement of the rule that could be checked against the chain's first entry, which is stronger than the current arrangement where the name and the value are the same expression.

- **The walk reuses the nearest probe by comparing `dir === nearestPath`, a string comparison standing in for "this is the first iteration"** — it is correct because the chain has no duplicates, which is true and is stated nowhere and is exactly the property a future de-duplication or normalisation pass would be added to guarantee. `chain.entries()` and an index test would say what is meant; a case pinning that the chain never repeats a link would defend what is currently assumed.

- **`probeNearestLink` and the walk both build a `ProbedLink` from a path and `probeChainLink`, and nothing says they build the same thing** — the two are the two ends of the same clause (the absent-install refusal probes fresh, the global-folder one carries the walk's answer), and a divergence between them — one realpathing, one not; one keeping the asked-about path, one the resolved one — would show up as two refusals describing the same checkout differently. One constructor used by both, or a case that runs both against one planted root and compares, would tie them.

- **`ChainLinks` is a `readonly` tuple and the walk copies it into a mutable array to build it, so the immutability stops at the return** — every current reader only iterates, which is why nothing has noticed. The value is that a reader who wants to sort or filter has to copy first, and the type says so; the gap is that `[nearest, ...rest]` rebuilds the list one entry at a time for the sole purpose of persuading the compiler, which is a cost paid on every refusal path and every call in the new cases.

### Probed-link follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **`ChainProvenance.nearest` is set on the first iteration of a loop and nothing says it is the NEAREST** — `nearest ??= { path: dir, link }` is correct only because `nodeModulesChain` returns the chain nearest-first, which is a property of a different function stated in its own doc comment. A reordering there (sorting, deduplicating, filtering out roots) would silently make this the wrong link, and every assertion about the clause would keep passing because the fixtures have a one-link chain. Asking the chain for its first entry explicitly, or asserting the invariant where the field is filled, would tie the two together.

- **Two refusals now get their nearest link from different sources and nothing compares them** — the global-folder message reads the walk's probe and the absent-install message probes fresh, which is right (there is no walk in the second case) and completely untested as a distinction: both fixtures have a nearest link in the same state, so a wiring swap between the two call sites passes everything. A case where the two would disagree — the walk's answer stale by construction — is hard to arrange, but asserting that the absent-install path calls the probe AT ALL (a counting seam, as `readdir` now is) would catch the swap.

- **`emptyLinkFrom` takes the walk's answer and the walk still probes every ancestor link it will never report on** — the finding is deliberately about the nearest link only, so the `statSync`/`realpathSync` pair paid for links two and beyond serves the provenance question exclusively. That is fine today and it is also why the memoisation suggestion standing since 2026-08-15 keeps being worth doing: thirteen guards per suite, each walking from a scratch root to the filesystem root, is the cost being paid for one boolean.

- **`probeNearestLink` is unexported and the only branch it owns — an empty chain — is asserted through `emptyLinkFrom(null)` instead** — that reads as coverage of the same case and is not: `emptyLinkFrom` is being told there is no link, while the claim worth pinning is that `probeNearestLink` RETURNS null for a root whose chain is empty. The filesystem root is the fixture and it needs no plant at all.

- **The `readdir` seam is typed `(path: string) => string[]`, which the real `fs.readdirSync` satisfies only in its no-options overload** — the same narrowing the `lstat` seam had before it was widened to `fs.Stats`: a probe that grows a `withFileTypes` call (the natural next step for telling a package directory from a stray file) compiles against `fs` and breaks every injected row at once. Typing it as the overload actually used, or switching the rows to `Dirent`-shaped fakes, would keep the seam honest about what it stands in for.


### Clause-builder follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **`renderedIn` proves a row is printable and not that any real walk prints it** — the corpus is built by calling the clause builder once per key, which is a loop written in the same file as the assertion: it shows the renderer can emit the row, not that a probe can ever produce the finding the row is about. The gap is narrow and it is the one that matters for a sketched-ahead kind, since whoever adds one will add the map entry too. Deriving the corpus from findings a PROBE returned — the fixtures for both obstruction kinds already exist — would close it.

- **The three clause builders are pure and exported and the paragraph that orders them is still three interpolations at two throw sites** — `emptyLinkClause` before `obstructionClause` in the global-folder message, neither in the unreadable-link one, and the order is where each was typed rather than a decision anyone made. Now that all three are functions of their findings, one `clauses(...)` taking the findings and returning the whole tail would put the ordering and the "which refusal gets which" rule in one readable place, which is the standing `loaderRefusal(kind, facts)` suggestion arriving from the other direction.

- **`nearestEmptyLink` is now called at both throw sites and still re-probes a link the walk already probed** — moving the probe out of the clause made the duplicate visible: `resolvedFromCheckout` runs `probeChainLink` on the nearest link during the walk, and each throw site runs it again while assembling the message. The two can disagree — an install landing between them is what a slow `npm ci` in another terminal produces — so the refusal can describe a state that no longer held when the decision was made. Carrying the nearest link's probe out of the walk in `ChainProvenance` would make the sentence report the walk it is about.

- **`unreadableList` renders a list and the count beside it is still interpolated at the throw site** — they agree by construction today, which the existing case notes, and the split is exactly where a de-dup or a truncation would be added to one side only. Returning the count with the list (or rendering the whole "N of the directories … — <list>" fragment in the builder) would make the agreement structural rather than a property of two lines staying next to each other.

- **`emptyLinkClause` takes `EmptyLink | null` and the null case is the caller's "nothing to say", so every future clause builder has to invent the same convention** — `obstructionClause` spells it as an empty array, `unreadableList` as an empty array, this one as `null`, and each is right for its own finding while none of them is stated as the rule. A shared `clause(finding, render)` — or simply the same emptiness convention across the three — would stop the fourth builder picking one at random.


### Syscall-attribution follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **The finding names the syscall and still prints the LINK as its path, including for the `lstat` row whose whole point is that a different directory is implicated** — the sentence now says "its PARENT directory could not be read", and then leaves the reader to derive which directory that is from a path it prints in full beside it. For a nested checkout the parent is not obvious at a glance, and it is exactly the argument `path.dirname` would produce. Carrying the implicated path on the finding — the link for `realpath`, `path.dirname(link)` for `lstat` — would let the message name the directory to fix instead of describing it.

- **`probeResolvedLink` is exported for its table and `probeChainLink`, which is the only caller, is not** — so the two halves of one probe have different reachability: the resolved half has rows for its race and its absence codes, and the half that decides WHICH half runs is still reachable only by planting a filesystem and spawning a child. The interesting rows there are cheap and currently uncovered — a `realpath` failure routed to the unreadable list versus one routed to `probeAbsentLink` — and they are the branch that decides whether a reader is told "cannot tell" or "not there".

- **Three `describeResolveFailure` catches now differ only in the syscall they tag, and each spells out the same four lines** — `probeChainLink`, `probeResolvedLink` and `probeAbsentLink` each call it, compare against null, and build the same `{ path, detail, syscall }`. That is the shape a helper takes: `unreadableFrom(syscall, path, error)` returning the finding or null would make "a non-absence code becomes an unreadable finding" one decision rather than three copies that agree today.

- **`SYSCALL_IMPLICATION.stat` explains a race and the message around it still says "could not be read", which is the one thing that did not happen** — the sentence's opening counts the links that "could not be read", and a `stat` failure means the link resolved fine and then stopped answering. The count is right and the verb is not, for the one row of three where the finding is about time rather than permissions. A per-syscall opening, or a verb chosen from the same table, would stop the paragraph contradicting its own parenthesis.

- **The `fs.Stats` fixtures are built once at suite load, from a directory that is deleted before the first case runs** — that is deliberate and it works (a `Stats` is a snapshot), but it also means a row can never ask a question about the path those stats came from, and nothing says so. A comment is not the fix; taking the stats inside a per-case helper that keeps its directory for the life of the case would let a future row read `.mode` or `.ino` against something real, which is the whole reason the seam now asks for the full shape.


### Phrase-rulebook follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **`assertPhraseTable` pins how a row READS and still says nothing about whether it is ever printed** — this is the same gap the obstruction table has carried since it was closed, now inherited by two more tables through the shared helper: a row added by someone sketching ahead (`"socket"`, a third `EmptyLinkHost`) satisfies every rule, ships as dead prose, and reads to the next person as a finding the walk knows how to make. The helper is the natural place to take an optional set of rendered messages and require each row to appear in one of them — the fixtures that render all three refusals already exist, and every table it guards is rendered by the same suite.

- **The template each table declares is a string the helper only quotes back, so a row can satisfy every rule against a sentence the fixture stopped rendering** — `template: "<path> <phrase>, so node had nothing to search in it"` is documentation with a type, checked by nobody: the clause it names lives in `guard-fixture.ts` and can be reworded without touching it, at which point the failure messages describe a sentence that no longer exists and the `opens` regex is pinned to the wrong grammar. Rendering the row INTO the declared template and asserting the result appears in a real refusal would make the template a claim rather than a caption.

- **`looksLikePackageEntry` is the resolver's rule and the walk that looks for `tsx` does not use it** — the predicate now states "a specifier is a plain name or a `@scope`", and one directory away `resolvedFromCheckout` joins `TSX_SPECIFIER` onto each link by hand, which is the same rule spelled as a constant that happens to be undotted. Nothing breaks today; what is missing is the link between them, so a future probe that looks for a scoped specifier (`@company/loader`) gets the path join right by luck. Asking the predicate to validate the specifier the walk is about to search for would tie the two to one rule.

- **The `EmptyLinkHost` union and the two throw sites that supply it are matched by hand** — `emptyLinkClause(root, "absent-install")` and `emptyLinkClause(root, "global-folder")` are string literals typed at the call sites, and swapping them compiles, passes the phrase-table rules, and produces two refusals each carrying the other's reading. Only the behavioural case catches it, and only because it renders both. Naming the host where the refusal itself is built — one `refusal(host, ...)` builder per message, which the standing `loaderRefusal(kind, facts)` suggestion already proposes — would make the pairing structural rather than a thing two interpolations remember.

- **The clause is now assembled from three tables and one hard-coded sentence, and only the tables are guarded** — "an interrupted `npm ci`, a pruned store or a cleaned cache is what leaves that behind" sits inline in `emptyLinkClause`, after the tail and before the period, and it is the only fragment of that sentence no rule applies to. It is also the fragment most likely to be reworded, since it is the one carrying the advice. Moving it beside the tables it is rendered with would put the whole sentence under the rulebook rather than two thirds of it.


### Package-counting follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **A `node_modules` holding one undotted directory is called healthy, whatever is in it** — the probe stops at "some entry is not a dotfile", so a store restore that got as far as `node_modules/ansi-styles` and died reports nothing at all, which is the same silence the entry-count version gave the bookkeeping-only case. The finding is about whether the checkout's install is usable, and one package is as far from that as zero. Reporting the COUNT of undotted entries when it is implausibly small (or when the manifest's own dependency list is an order of magnitude longer) would reach the case that is currently the last one left invisible.

- **The undotted-name rule is stated in a doc comment and enforced by an inline predicate at one call site** — "a specifier is a plain name or a `@scope`, never a leading dot" is a fact about node's resolution that this file now depends on in two places (this probe, and the `tsx` entry the walk looks for), and only one of them says it. A named `looksLikePackageEntry(entry)` would give the rule one home and let the interesting rows — `@scope`, `.bin`, `.package-lock.json`, a plain name — be a table rather than a fixture per shape.

- **The two phrasing tables now have different rules for the same property and nothing says why** — `OBSTRUCTION_PHRASE` forbids a period anywhere, `EMPTY_LINK_PHRASE` forbids one only at the end, because the latter legitimately names `.package-lock.json`. That is a real reason and it lives nowhere; the next table copied from either one inherits a rule by coin flip. One shared `assertPhraseTable(table)` helper, with the punctuation rule as a parameter and the reason beside it, would make the difference a decision.

- **`nearestEmptyLink` reads the whole directory listing to answer a question that stops at the first undotted entry** — a healthy `node_modules` holds hundreds of entries and every one is allocated before `some` looks at any of them. `opendirSync` with a loop that returns on the first undotted name reads one entry in the common case, and the function is one edit away from being called per chain link rather than once.

- **The clause's tail is one sentence appended to two different refusals and it asserts something only one of them makes true** — "this refusal reads exactly like one for a checkout with no `node_modules` at all" is precisely right in the absent-install message and false in the global-folder one, which opens by naming a resolved path from `$NODE_PATH` and reads nothing like the absent case. The tail was written for the first and carried to the second by the same interpolation. Splitting the tail per refusal — or dropping it where it does not hold — would stop the message making a claim the reader can check and disprove.

### Empty-link follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **`nearestEmptyLink` re-probes a chain link the provenance walk has already probed, one syscall later and with no memory of the answer** — `resolvedFromCheckout` calls `probeChainLink` on the nearest link during the walk, and the clause builder calls it again on the same path while assembling the message. The two can disagree (an install landing between them is exactly the race a slow `npm ci` in another terminal produces), so the refusal can name a state that no longer held when the decision was made. Carrying the nearest link's probe out of the walk in `ChainProvenance` would make the sentence report the walk the refusal is about.

- **The empty check runs `readdirSync` and throws the whole listing away to compare one length** — a `node_modules` in a real checkout holds hundreds of entries, and every one of them is allocated as a string to answer "is this zero". `opendirSync` plus a single `readSync` answers the same question in one entry; on the refusal path this costs nothing today, but the function is one edit away from being called per chain link rather than once.

- **"Empty" here means zero entries, and a `node_modules` holding only `.package-lock.json` is the same failure wearing a hat** — npm writes that file first and the store restore can fail after it, leaving a directory that is not empty by `readdirSync` and holds nothing node can load. The reader is then back to the refusal this finding was created to replace, with the extra confusion that the message explicitly declined to call it empty. Reporting "holds nothing loadable" — no entry that is not a dotfile — would match what the sentence is trying to say.

- **Three clause builders now append to the same two messages and their order is positional** — `emptyLinkClause` was inserted before `obstructionClause` in the global-folder refusal because that is where the interpolation was typed, and nothing states that "why your own directory said nothing" should precede "what is standing in the chain". A fourth clause will be placed by the same coin flip. One `clauses(...findings)` that orders and joins them would make the paragraph's shape a decision rather than an artifact of editing.

- **The two negative cases assert `doesNotMatch(/is empty/)` against prose the fixture owns, so a reworded clause makes them vacuous rather than red** — the positive cases have the same shape (`/is there and is empty/`), and this is the failure `OBSTRUCTION_PHRASE` was extracted to stop for the obstruction sentences one commit earlier. The clause's wording wants the same treatment: one exported fragment the cases read, so a rewrite either updates the pins or fails them.

### Obstruction-table follow-ups (2026-08-16)

- **Nothing ties an `ObstructionKind` to a probe that can emit it** — the new table pins each row's shape (verb phrase, no punctuation, distinct from its siblings), which is what stops a badly-worded row, and says nothing about whether the row is ever PRODUCED. A kind added by someone sketching ahead ("socket", "no-execute-bit") passes every assertion, ships as dead prose, and reads to the next person as a finding the walk knows how to make. A sweep requiring each key to appear in some refusal this suite renders — the two fixtures already exist — would make the table's rows claims rather than intentions.

- **The injected `lstat` is typed structurally as `{ isSymbolicLink(): boolean }`, so the next field the probe reads breaks every row silently** — `fs.lstatSync` satisfies that type and so does a two-line stub, which is the point; what the narrow type also does is let `probeAbsentLink` grow a `.isFile()` or a `.mode` check that compiles against the real syscall and throws `is not a function` on all five table rows at once. Typing the parameter `typeof fs.lstatSync` costs nothing at the call sites (the stubs would grow to `fs.Stats`-shaped fakes, or the rows would use `fs.lstatSync.bind(fs)` against a planted path) and makes the seam refuse a stub the production path has outgrown.

- **A `statSync` failure inside `probeChainLink` is reported as a `realpath` failure** — the second syscall sits in the same `try`, so a target removed between the two calls (or a filesystem that answers `realpath` and then refuses `stat`) lands in the catch that exists to classify the FIRST one, and the reader gets an errno attributed to a syscall that succeeded. The window is a race and the message is the only artifact of it; catching the `stat` separately, or naming the syscall in `UnreadableLink`, would stop the report pointing at the wrong call.

- **`UnreadableLink.detail` now has two possible sources and renders identically for both** — an `EACCES` reaching the list from `realpath` means the chain link itself could not be traversed; the same code reaching it from the new `lstat` catch means its PARENT could not be read, which is a different directory to fix. The refusal prints `<path> (EACCES)` either way, and the path it prints is the link in both cases, so the reader is sent to `chmod` the thing that is not the problem. One more field on the finding — the syscall, or the directory actually implicated — would keep the two apart in the sentence.

- **`obstructionClause` is pure, is not exported, and the negative case pays two child-process boots to assert that it returns the empty string** — "no findings in, no clause out" is a one-row table, and it is currently proved by spawning node twice with a planted `NODE_PATH` and a symlink cycle, because the only way to reach the function is through a refusal. Exporting it would leave the spawns for the claim they are genuinely needed for (the clause reaches the message a real walk produced) and move the emptiness, the singular/plural split and the one-versus-many list into rows with no filesystem at all.

### Obstruction-reporting follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **`ChainObstruction.what` is a free-text fragment written at two throw-adjacent sites, and the clause reads it into a sentence** — "is not a directory" and "is a symlink whose target does not exist" are grammatical only because both were written to fit the one template that renders them; a third shape (a socket, a FIFO, a directory with no execute bit) added by someone reading only the probe would produce a sentence that does not parse. A closed union of kinds with the phrasing in one table — the shape `PROBLEM_DETAIL` already has in `lib/scanned-floor.ts` — would keep the sentence a property of the table.
