- **The obstruction walk stops at the first thing in the way per link and never asks what is INSIDE a readable one** — a `node_modules` directory that exists, is readable, and is EMPTY (a pruned store, an interrupted `npm ci`) is the most common real version of "the install is not where you think", and it produces no finding at all: the walk asks only whether `tsx` is under it. One `readdirSync` on the nearest link, reported when it comes back empty, would name the case the reader hits most often.

- **`danglingLink` swallows every `lstat` failure as "not there"** — an `EACCES` on the PARENT directory makes `lstat` throw for a link that may well exist, and the catch reports the ordinary-absence answer, which is the same collapse the unreadable-link finding was created to stop, one level down. Reusing `describeResolveFailure` in that catch (and routing a non-absence code into the unreadable list) would keep the two questions apart in both places.

- **Three findings now share one walk and each has its own array** — `unreadable`, `obstructed` and the returned `fromCheckout` are assembled by hand and the refusal picks between them with two `if`s at the throw site. A single `findings: ChainFinding[]` discriminated on kind, with the refusal rendering whichever kinds are present, would make "every finding reaches the message" structural rather than a thing three call sites currently happen to do.

- **The negative case asserts on the absent-install refusal only, and the clause is appended to two other messages** — a regression that made the clause unconditional in the GLOBAL_FOLDERS or unreadable-link message would leave the new case green, because that message is not the one it reads. Asserting the absence across all three refusals — the trio the distinctness case already renders — would cost one loop and cover the paths the clause actually lands in.

### Chain-walk follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **`notDirectoryClause` and the unreadable-link list build their prose separately, and both refusals now end in an appended sentence** — the clause is rendered from a table of one, the link list from a `map`+`join` at the throw site, and the two agree on tone by hand. That is the same shape as the standing `loaderRefusal(kind, facts)` suggestion, now with a third fragment behind it: one renderer taking the findings and producing the whole paragraph would make "every refusal names every finding" a property of the table rather than of three call sites remembering to interpolate.

- **The count in the unreadable-link sentence is rendered from `provenance.unreadable.length` and the list from the same array one line later, so the new agreement assertion holds by construction rather than by check** — that is worth saying out loud, because the assertion reads as if it were guarding a genuine risk and today it guards a rewrite that has not happened. The version with teeth compares the count against the number of DISTINCT paths in the rendered message, which would also catch a de-dup added to one side and not the other.

- **`statSync` is called on every readable chain link, on every guard spawn, and the answer for the ancestors above the checkout never changes** — the walk already pays a `realpathSync` per link and now pays a `statSync` beside it, from the root of every scratch fixture up to the filesystem root, thirteen guards per suite. Memoising the probe per absolute path for the life of the process (the suites do not create and destroy the same path twice) would cut it to one pair per distinct directory, and the memo is a plain `Map` because the answers are only read within one run.

### Thrown-value rendering follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **`lib/thrown-value.ts` is pure and nothing keeps it that way** — it is imported by a `scripts/` guard helper and by a `__tests__/helpers/` fixture, and its whole value is that it can be called anywhere, including inside a `catch` that is already handling a filesystem failure. The next edit reaching for `fs` or `process.env` inside it (to stat a path named in the error, to check a `DEBUG` flag) would compile, pass every existing case, and turn one of the two call sites into a second failure source. The `lint-guards` fleet already refuses spawn shapes in `__tests__/*.test.ts`; a sibling rule refusing `node:fs`/`node:child_process`/`process.` in this module would state the property its two callers rest on.

- **The 200-character cap slices UTF-16 units, so a truncated emoji or a CJK name can end in half a character** — `rendered.slice(0, THROWN_LIMIT)` cuts by code unit, and a surrogate pair straddling the boundary leaves a lone surrogate that a terminal renders as a replacement glyph. The value being rendered is arbitrary user-ish data (a path, a package name, a config string), so this is reachable rather than theoretical. Slicing by code point — `[...rendered].slice(0, LIMIT).join("")` — costs one line and removes the class.

- **`describeThrownReason` prefers `code` over everything, including a message that says more** — an `ENOENT` is the whole story and the errno is the right answer; an `ERR_INVALID_ARG_TYPE` is a code whose message carries the argument, the expected type and the received one, all of which are dropped. The two live in the same field and the split between them is "does the code name a filesystem state or a programming error". Reporting `code: message` for the non-errno codes would keep the searchable part and stop discarding the part the reader needs.

- **Twelve wrappers report an unreadable input and no test spawns one that is genuinely unreadable** — `readTextInput`'s catch is reached in the suites by patching or by a missing file (which is `ENOENT`, the easy row); the interesting ones — a directory where a file is declared (`EISDIR`), a file the runner cannot read (`EACCES`), a JSON parse failure — reach `assertParsedInputs` through a path nothing exercises end to end. The guard fixture already knows how to plant a scratch root; one wrapper run against a declared input that is a directory would pin the sentence a reader actually gets.

- **`asText`'s refusal interpolates `value.reason` and is the one message that can now carry 200 characters of a thrown config tree** — it is the wiring-mistake guard ("an unreadable input reached a reader anyway"), so its whole job is to name the programming error, and the thrown value is the least relevant part of that sentence. Now that the reason can be a rendered object rather than a bare errno, this message inherits a width it was never written for. Naming the input and dropping the reason — or capping it far shorter here than in the report the reason exists for — would keep the wiring error legible.

### Distinctness-and-detail follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **`describeResolveFailure` is exported from a file five suites import for other reasons, with no guard that it stays pure** — it is the one function here safe to call in a table, and the next edit that reaches for `fs` inside it would silently turn every row into a filesystem test without failing anything. The `lint-guards` fleet already refuses spawn shapes in `__tests__/*.test.ts`; a sibling rule refusing `fs.`/`spawn` inside this function would keep the property its whole value rests on.

- **The three loader refusals are built inline at three throw sites, so their shared vocabulary is copied prose** — "the checkout", "`node_modules` directories node would search", "the spawn below" appear in two or three of them, retyped each time, which is how the first one drifted. A small `loaderRefusal(kind, facts)` rendering all three from one place — the pattern `FAILURE_MESSAGE` already sets in `lib/scanned-floor.ts` — would make the distinctness case a property of the table rather than of three literals.

### Errno-reporting follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **The unreadable-link refusal is decided by `unreadable.length > 0`, so ONE unrelated bad link anywhere up the chain hides the global-folder finding** — the walk goes to the filesystem root, so a stale `/tmp/node_modules` symlink cycle (or an `EACCES` on a directory in someone's home) planted by anything at all makes every genuinely-global resolution report "could not be decided" instead of naming GLOBAL_FOLDERS. Reporting only the links that could plausibly have CONTAINED the resolved path — or naming both findings in one sentence when both apply — would stop an unrelated link two directories from the root from suppressing the diagnosis the reader needs.

### Containment-check follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **The provenance check is a pure function of two paths and is not exported, so its table has to be written through a process spawn** — every case that pins it (`NODE_PATH`, the symlinked install, the hoisted ancestor, the unloadable entry) costs a scratch tree and, for two of them, a full node + tsx boot. Exporting `resolvedFromCheckout` would let the interesting rows — a chain link that is a file rather than a directory, a resolved path that is a PREFIX-sibling (`…/node_modules_old/tsx`), an ancestor at the filesystem root — be a table with no filesystem at all, leaving the spawns for the one end-to-end claim.

- **`isUnder` is a string comparison and the resolved path's case is the filesystem's business** — on a case-insensitive volume (macOS's default, Windows) a chain of `/Users/me/Repo/node_modules` and a resolution of `/Users/me/repo/node_modules/tsx/dist/loader.mjs` do not share a prefix, and the check refuses an install that is plainly the checkout's own. `fs.realpathSync.native` normalises case on those platforms where plain `realpathSync` does not; using it (or comparing case-insensitively when `process.platform` says so) would keep the refusal for the case it was written for.

- **Every case in this suite builds its scratch trees by hand, and the fake global package is now written twice** — `mkdirSync` + a `package.json` + an `index.js`, spelled out in two cases that differ only in what else is on the chain. A `plantPackage(dir, name)` helper in `helpers/guard-fixture.ts` would let the next case say what it is testing (an empty entry, a broken symlink, a manifest with no `main`) instead of restating how a package is shaped.

### Provenance-check follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **`nodeModulesChain` walks to the filesystem root, so `/tmp/node_modules` counts as a checkout's own install** — the chain is every ancestor, which is what node walks, but "the checkout owns this install" is a narrower claim than "some ancestor could have provided it": a scratch root under `/tmp` accepts a world-writable `/tmp/node_modules`, and a checkout under `$HOME` accepts one in the home directory, which is a global folder by every meaning except node's. Bounding the walk at the nearest enclosing `package.json` — or at the git root — would match what a reader takes the sentence to mean.

- **The global-folder case pays a full node + tsx boot to test one environment variable** — it is the only spawn in this suite that is not a guard run, and it exists because `NODE_PATH` is read once at startup. The rule it proves (`resolvedFromCheckout`) is a pure function of two paths and is not exported, so the cheap table-driven test of the same rule cannot be written. Exporting the predicate and giving it a handful of synthetic paths would move most of the coverage off the spawn and leave the child for the single end-to-end claim.

- **The hoisted-install case creates a temp directory inside the repository, and cleans it up in a `finally` that a crash outruns** — every guard floor is a count over this tree, so a `guard-fixture-nested-*` left behind is a directory the next `lint:all` walks and the next `npm ci` never removes. A `finally` covers a failed assertion and not a killed process, which is exactly how the run that matters ends. Registering the cleanup with the runner (`t.after`) or sweeping stale `guard-fixture-nested-*` roots in the suite's `after` would make the removal survive the failure it is most likely to follow.

- **Both loader refusals end with "Run `npm ci`" and only one of them is fixed by it** — a checkout resolving through `$NODE_PATH` may have a perfectly good install behind it and a stale environment variable in front, and running `npm ci` again changes nothing. The message names the GLOBAL_FOLDERS rule but not the value that triggered it, so the reader has to guess which global folder answered. Printing `process.env.NODE_PATH` (and the resolved file's nearest `node_modules`) would turn the diagnosis into the fix.

### Loader-resolution follow-ups (2026-08-15)

- **The check resolves for the CJS condition and the spawn loads the ESM one** — `require.resolve` answered `node_modules/tsx/dist/loader.mjs` on this checkout, but `--import tsx` goes through the package's `import` condition, and nothing says the two entry points must both exist. A package that shipped only a `require` entry would pass the check and fail the spawn with the empty-pipes signature the check exists to prevent, one door over. `import.meta.resolve` (or a one-line child process doing the real `--import`) would test the path actually taken.

- **Thirteen guards are spawned per suite and the loader is resolved thirteen times** — `tsxLoader()` runs before every memo miss, so a resolution that is the same answer every time is paid once per guard per file. It is cheap (`require.resolve` caches internally) and it is also the one call whose failure must be seen EARLY rather than on whichever guard happens to run first; memoising it per root with the failure re-thrown would keep the diagnosis and drop the repeat.

- **The bin-spawn sweep reads `__tests__/*.test.ts` and the helpers are where a spawn would actually be written** — the drift guard walks one directory level, so `__tests__/helpers/` is outside it, and a private spawn added to a helper (which is exactly where the last one lived before it was migrated) would not be seen. Widening the walk to the helper directory, or expressing the rule as a `LINT_GUARDS` entry over the whole test tree the way `check-analytics-imports` does, would put the rule where it applies rather than where it was written.

- **`stripComments` is imported from `lib/env-inlining.ts` by a suite that has nothing to do with env inlining** — the function is a general lexer and its home is a guard's private module, so the third caller will either import it from an unrelated guard again or write a fourth copy (`lib/check-problem-phrasing-imports.ts` already reaches across for it the same way). Moving it to a `lib/source-text.ts` beside the other pure text helpers would give the two guards and this sweep one lexer with one set of string-literal edge cases to be right about.

### Fixture-refusal follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **`tsxLoaderIn` takes a root and still hardcodes `node_modules/tsx` as the thing that proves an install** — the check is `existsSync` on a directory, so a half-written `node_modules` (an interrupted `npm ci`, a pruned store) passes it and the spawn fails anyway with the empty-pipes signature the check exists to prevent. Resolving the loader the way node will — `require.resolve("tsx", {paths: [root]})` — would test the thing the spawn actually depends on rather than a directory that shares its name.

- **The leak check reads `os.tmpdir()` by prefix, so two suites running concurrently would see each other's scratch roots** — `assertRefusesWithoutLeaking` diffs the set of `lint-guard-partial-*` directories around the throw, and node's test runner runs files in parallel processes. Today the diff is empty because the other suites create their roots outside the window, which is luck rather than isolation: a partial-root fixture built by a sibling file mid-assertion would be attributed to this one. Having `makePartialRoot` take an optional prefix, or recording created roots in a module-level set the helper exposes, would make the claim about THIS call rather than about the directory.

- **`runPatched` restores by rewriting pristine bytes and nothing checks the copy between suites** — the restore is now inside the `finally` where it belongs, but "restored" is still only ever asserted by the case that patched. A copy that drifted for any other reason (a guard that WRITES during a run — the generator-backed `--check` guards are one `--check` flag away from doing exactly that) would leave every later case in the file running against bytes nobody declared. A `verifyPristine()` on `SharedPatchedRepo`, called in the suite's `after`, would turn that from an assumption into a line.

- **`entryPatch`'s brace counter ignores braces inside strings, and `SCANNED_FLOORS` notes are prose** — the parser counts every `{` and `}` between the key and the close, including any that appear inside a `note:` string. No committed note contains one today, so the slice is right; a note that ever quoted a code snippet or a `${...}` would end the entry early and the rewrite would land in the wrong half — the exact failure the counter was written to prevent, arriving through the text it does not skip. A test that plants a braced note would say whether this matters before a note does.

- **Five suites now build their own `makeSharedPatchedRepo`, and each one costs a ~200-file copy** — this file makes a third. The per-case copy was already eliminated once by sharing within a suite; the same argument now applies across suites, and the restore discipline that would have to hold (a `finally` per run) already does. A module-scope copy in `helpers/` with reference counting would cut the fixture setup for the three slowest guard suites without changing a single assertion.

### Shared-fixture follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **`entryPatch` is exported from a helper and nothing keeps a third copy from being written** — the two harnesses import it now, but the reason the duplicate appeared in the first place (a function declared inside a `*.test.ts` file cannot be imported by a sibling suite) applies to every other private fixture helper in those files: `guardFor`, the `runFor` shape, the control-run builder. A guard over `__tests__/*.test.ts` refusing a `function` declaration that also exists in `helpers/` — or simply a rule that fixture helpers live in `helpers/` — would state the convention the two files now follow by accident.

- **The helper hardcodes `lib/scanned-floor.ts` in its messages and takes the source as an argument** — `entryPatch` never opens a file, so `ENTRY_TABLE_MODULE` is a claim about which file the CALLER will apply it to. A caller that used it on a different table (there is nothing stopping one) would get a diagnostic naming a module its patch never touched. Passing the module name in, or deriving it from the patch key `runPatched` is already given, would make the message a fact about the run.

- **Both harnesses build a `runFor(code)` that memoises through `runPatched`'s label, and the labels are hand-namespaced** — `problem-${code}` in one file and `lookup-${code}` in the other, with the prefix as the only thing keeping two runs of the same guard apart if the two suites ever share a `SharedPatchedRepo`. They do not today (each file makes its own copy), which is exactly why nothing catches a collision. Deriving the label from the patch itself — a hash of the rewritten bytes — would make the memo key a fact about what ran.

- **Two harnesses now copy `lib/` + `scripts/` at ~200 files each, once per suite** — the shared copy was introduced to stop a per-CASE copy, and the same argument applies one level up now that a second file wants one. A module-scope copy shared across suites would need the restore discipline to hold across files (it already does, in a `finally`), and would halve the fixture setup for the two slowest guard suites.

- **`makeSharedPatchedRepo`'s marker injection is asserted per case, and the injection itself is untested** — every case checks the copy spoke, which is the right claim; nothing checks the OPPOSITE, that a repo built without the marker fails loudly rather than quietly making every provenance assertion vacuous. The constructor throws when `lib/scanned-floor.ts` is not among the copied entries, and no test exercises that throw — so the one guard protecting thirteen provenance assertions has never been seen to fire.

### Lookup-code follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **`lookupFailure` zeroes `count`, `minimum` and `label`, and only a test says those fields are meaningless for the three codes** — the failure type is flat, so `{code: "no_floor_declared", count: 0, minimum: 0}` is indistinguishable at the type level from a walk that genuinely found nothing against a floor of nothing. The "says nothing about the tree" assertion is the only thing standing between a future message and a quoted zero. This is the same finding the standing discriminated-union suggestion names, now with three more codes behind it: nine codes on one flat object, six of which read `count`/`minimum` and three of which must not.

- **`no_floor_declared` is raised where a check name is looked up, and nothing asserts a guard cannot make its OWN name up** — the code exists because a rename leaves a wrapper asking for a key nobody committed, but a wrapper that computed its check name (`path.basename(__filename)`, an env var, `process.argv`) would produce this failure as ordinary behaviour rather than as a finding. That is the same premise `WRAPPER_UNREACHABLE` rests on one file over, still unasserted: one guard over `scripts/check-*.ts` requiring `scannedFloorFor`/`assertScannedFloor`/`assertParsedInputs` to be called with a string LITERAL would turn both excuses into something the tooling refuses to let drift.

- **The `no_inputs_floor` case has to date a note it is not testing** — patching `check-appstore-config` into a count-shaped entry trips `undated_note` unless the rewrite also inserts a date, so the fixture carries a second edit whose only job is to keep the validator quiet. That edit is a fact about `validateScannedFloorEntry`'s ORDER, written down in a harness that has nothing to do with note dating, and it goes stale silently if the order ever changes (the case would start asserting `undated_note`'s sentence and the "prints the whole refusal" assertion would fail in a file that never mentions notes). A fixture helper that asserts the patched entry validates CLEAN before the run would state the requirement instead of satisfying it by hand.

- **Two harnesses now carry the same entry-slicing brace counter** — `lint-guard-invalid-floor.test.ts`'s `entryPatch` and `lint-guard-missing-floor.test.ts`'s inline slicer are the same twenty lines: find `  "<key>": {`, count braces, rewrite inside, assert the rewrite changed something. The second copy was written rather than imported because the first lives in a `*.test.ts` file that the runner glob owns, not in `__tests__/helpers/`. Moving it to `helpers/guard-fixture.ts` beside `makeSharedPatchedRepo` would leave one copy of a parser both files depend on being right about.

- **Nine failure codes, and the split between "produced by an evaluator" and "produced by a lookup" is a hand-written `Extract`** — `EVALUATOR_PRODUCED` lists six codes and names a harness for each, which is stronger than a bare exemption, but the list itself is a claim about `lib/scanned-floor.ts` that the module does not make. A code moved from an evaluator to a lookup (or the reverse) would leave the record naming the wrong side while still compiling. Deriving the split from the source — the codes reachable from `evaluateScannedFloor`/`evaluateParsedInputs` versus those reachable from `lookupFailure` — would make it a fact about the module rather than a copy of one.

### Phrasing-guard follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **The guard proves a joiner is IN SCOPE, not that it was used** — a file importing `describeScannedFloorProblem` and then building `subject + " " + detail` two lines later passes cleanly. That is the deliberate limit (the cheap structural claim, same as `check-analytics-imports`), and it is worth saying that the stronger check is available: flagging a template literal or `+` expression whose operands are both accessor CALLS would catch the actual hand-join rather than its precondition, at the cost of a matcher that has to understand expressions.

- **Namespace reads are matched by regex over the whole stripped file, so a string mentioning `floor.scannedFloorProblemDetail` counts as a read** — comments are stripped, string literals are not. The failure mode is a false POSITIVE (a guard refusing on a file that only talks about the accessor in a string), which is the safe direction, but it is still a refusal nobody can act on. Skipping string literals is the same lexer the `entryPatch` brace-counter suggestion asks for, and would serve both.

- **Thirteen guards, and `PARTIAL_FIXTURES` had to be edited by hand for the new one** — the partial-root harness refuses a count-shaped guard with no fixture, which is what caught the omission here; but the fixture itself (`() => ["app"]`) is a fact about which roots the guard walks, and that fact already lives in the guard's source as `SCANNED_DIRS`. Exporting the scan roots from each guard module — or moving them into the `SCANNED_FLOORS` entry beside the floor — would let the harness derive "the smallest root" instead of being told it, and would delete a per-guard hand-kept list.

- **The floor is 450 against 638 files, and `__tests__/` is 62% of the walk** — the property the note claims (no single root clears it) holds today because tests outnumber everything else; it stops holding the moment `__tests__/` alone passes 450, which at the current rate is a few months of ordinary test-writing. Unlike the other count floors, this one gets WEAKER as the repo grows healthily. A floor expressed as a fraction of the largest root, or a test asserting `max(root) < minimum`, would notice the day the claim in the note stops being true.

- **`PROBLEM_SENTENCE_JOINERS` lists four names and nothing ties it to the module's actual exports** — a joiner renamed in `lib/scanned-floor.ts` leaves this list naming a function that no longer exists, and the guard silently gets stricter (every file importing the renamed joiner starts failing) or looser (if the rename split one joiner into two). A test importing each name from the module — or a `typeof` check against the real exports — would make the list a claim about the module rather than a copy of it.

### Blank-key follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **`WRAPPER_UNREACHABLE` is an excuse a future reader cannot re-check** — the record says a blank `SCANNED_FLOORS` key can never reach a wrapper because every guard asks for its floor under a string literal in its own source. That is true today and nothing asserts it: a wrapper that computed its check name (from `process.argv`, from its filename, from an env var) would make the excuse false and no test in the repository would notice. One guard over `scripts/check-*.ts` requiring `scannedFloorFor`/`assertScannedFloor`/`assertParsedInputs` to be called with a literal — the shape `check-analytics-imports` already uses for import sites — would turn the excuse into something the tooling refuses to let drift.

- **`empty_check_name` is validated per entry, and the property is about the TABLE** — `validateScannedFloorEntry` reports it for the one key it is handed, so `validateScannedFloors` catches a blank key only because it happens to iterate every entry. The neighbouring property — two entries under keys that differ by whitespace or case (`"check-secrets"` and `"check-secrets "`) — has no code at all, and it is the same class of bug: a key nobody can look up, sitting in the table looking covered. A table-level pass beside the per-entry one would hold both, and would be the natural home for `duplicate_check_name`.

- **Eleven problem codes, ten wrapper runs, and the split is now a type** — `ProblemCodeKey = Exclude<…>` is honest about `empty_check_name`, but it also creates a second, easier way for a code to ship without a wrapper run: write a paragraph. The reason field is prose, so nothing distinguishes "structurally unreachable" from "I did not want to build the fixture". A stronger form would make the excuse mechanical — an assertion that the code cannot be produced by `validateScannedFloorEntry` for ANY floor when the check name is a non-blank literal — which for this code is exactly true and provable in three lines.

- **The clause for `empty_check_name` reads correctly in the entry's voice and oddly in the key's** — through `scannedFloorFor` it renders "its SCANNED_FLOORS entry is keyed by a blank name…", which is right; through `formatScannedFloorProblem` it renders " is keyed by a blank name…", which is the sentence describing its own defect. That is deliberate and it is also the only clause in the table whose two renderings differ in quality. A per-code choice of subject — some problems are about the entry, some about the key — would fix it properly, at the cost of a second axis on a table that just got one.

- **`scannedFloorFor` still throws a bare `Error` for an unknown check name, beside a `ScannedFloorError` for an unusable one** — a guard asking for a floor nobody committed gets a message with no `ERROR —` prefix, no check-name head, and a stack trace, because the wrapper only special-cases `ScannedFloorError`. The two failures are the same class from the reader's side ("this guard's premise is not usable"), and the missing-entry one is the MORE likely of the two after a rename. Giving it a failure code — or throwing `ScannedFloorError` with a `no_floor_declared` code — would make the two refusals print alike.

### Subject-id follow-ups (2026-08-14)

- **`problem` is optional on a flat failure type, and only a test says which codes may carry it** — `ScannedFloorFailure` declares `input?`, `reason?` and `problem?` on one object, so `{code: "no_files", problem: {...}}` type-checks and the new "attaches a problem to invalid_floor and to nothing else" case is the only thing standing against it. A discriminated union on `code` would make each variant carry exactly its own fields and delete three optionals plus the `f.input`/`f.reason` non-null reads in `FAILURE_MESSAGE`. It touches twelve wrappers and every harness that builds a failure by hand, which is why it was not done here — but it is the version where the invariant is a type rather than an assertion.

- **The numeric `invalid_floor` and the ten structural ones share a code and answer to different readers** — one says "the number you passed is not a floor" (a caller bug, no entry to look at), the other says "your SCANNED_FLOORS row is unusable" (a table bug). They print from the same branch, both end with `REMEASURE_HINT`, and only the presence of `problem` tells them apart. Splitting `invalid_floor` into `invalid_minimum_argument` and `invalid_entry` would let each carry the fields it actually needs, would delete the optional, and would let the remeasure hint be a per-code property — but it is a public failure code with a ten-case wrapper harness keyed on it, so the cost is real and the win is legibility.

- **`scannedFloorProblemSubject` accepts an id and returns words, and nothing stops a caller re-joining them by hand** — the repo now has three functions between a code and a sentence (`scannedFloorProblemDetail`, `describeScannedFloorProblem`, `describeScannedFloorProblemRef`) and the drift guard is one regex over `lib/scanned-floor.ts` per private table. Any other module can import the subject accessor and the detail accessor and build its own `"<subject> <clause>"`, and no assertion in the repository would notice — the same gap the standing "repo-wide guard for `PROBLEM_DETAIL` readers" suggestion names, now with a second table behind it. One `check-*` guard asserting that a file importing either accessor also imports a describe function would state the rule where it applies.

- **The two subject ids are `"entry"` and `"caller"`, and `formatScannedFloorProblem` still passes a raw check name as a third subject** — that call site is the ordinary one (a test listing every problem in the table), so the id union deliberately does not cover it and `describeScannedFloorProblem` still takes a bare string. The consequence is that the union is not the full set of subjects the module speaks about, only the set that travels on a failure, and nothing says so where a reader would look. A doc line on the union — or a `ScannedFloorProblemSubject | {checkName: string}` shape — would stop the next reader from assuming the two ids are exhaustive over the module's voices.

- **A blank subject still renders a sentence with a missing first word** — carried over from the previous round and now one layer deeper: `describeScannedFloorProblem("", code)` yields `" declares no premise…"`, and the ref path cannot produce it (both ids resolve to non-empty constants, which the new test pins) while the `formatScannedFloorProblem` path can, if an entry key is ever empty. `validateScannedFloorEntry` does not check its own `checkName`, so an empty key in `SCANNED_FLOORS` is invisible to the validator and visible only as a sentence that starts with a space. One `if (!checkName.trim()) add("empty_check_name")` closes it at the source instead of at the renderer.

### Two-field-problem follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **`ScannedFloorFailure.detail` is the same field, on the other type, and it survived** — the problem type just lost its rendered-sentence field because every renderer derived the words from `code` anyway; the FAILURE type still carries `detail?: string`, and `FAILURE_MESSAGE.invalid_floor` prints it verbatim. So the sentence a guard shows for `invalid_floor` is still chosen by whichever call site built the failure, which is exactly the state the problem type was in an hour ago. Carrying the problem CODE on the failure instead would let `FAILURE_MESSAGE` render it like the other five codes, and would delete the `?? NUMERIC_FLOOR_DETAIL(f.minimum)` fallback rather than leaving it unreached.

- **The `Object.keys` assertion pins the problem's shape at runtime and the type is what callers actually see** — `assert.deepEqual(Object.keys(problem).sort(), ["checkName", "code"])` catches a producer that starts attaching a words field; it does not catch a TYPE that declares one and is never populated, and it runs over problems from one hand-built entry rather than over every code. Deriving it from the same exhaustive `EVERY_CODE` record the phrasing block already declares would make it a claim about the type's whole surface instead of about one fixture.

- **`describeScannedFloorProblem` takes `subject` as a bare string and two of the three call sites pass an exported constant** — the third passes `problem.checkName`, so the parameter cannot be narrowed to a union without losing the ordinary case. It can still be made harder to misuse: a blank subject renders `" declares no premise…"`, a leading space and no subject at all, which is what a caller passing an empty check name would print today. One `if (!subject.trim()) throw` would make that a loud bug instead of a sentence with a missing first word.

- **Nothing asserts the ten clauses are grammatically composable with a subject** — `describeScannedFloorProblem` joins them with a space, so every clause has to start with a verb phrase (`declares…`, `carries…`, `delegates…`) for the result to read as English. They all do, by convention, and a new code written as `"the entry has no note"` would render `check-x the entry has no note` and pass every assertion in the file. A per-clause check that the first word is not an article or a pronoun is crude and would catch exactly this.

- **`REMEASURE_HINT` is appended to four of six failure messages and omitted from two, with the reasoning in a comment on only one of them** — `no_files` deliberately does NOT carry it (the note says why: pointing that reader at the number is how a floor gets lowered to whatever a broken walk returns), and `empty_input`/`unreadable_input` omit it with no comment at all. Whether that omission is the same deliberate choice or an oversight cannot be told from the file. Making the hint a declared property per code — a record beside `FAILURE_MESSAGE`, exhaustive like it — would force each new code to answer the question rather than inherit whatever the author last pasted.

### Single-phrasing follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **The source-text drift guard is the only thing standing between four call sites and five** — `one problem, one phrasing` asserts `PROBLEM_DETAIL` has exactly one reader by regex over `lib/scanned-floor.ts`, which catches a new reader IN THAT FILE and nothing else. Any other module can import `scannedFloorProblemDetail` and build its own `"<subject> <clause>"` string, and no assertion in the repository would notice. A repo-wide guard — every file that imports the accessor must also import `describeScannedFloorProblem`, or must be a test — would state the rule where it actually applies, in the shape `check-analytics-imports` already uses for the analytics module.

- **Five sentences in `PROBLEM_DETAIL` explain their own rule and five just name the defect** — `invalid_minimum`, `duplicate_input`, `delegation_with_shape`, `empty_delegation` and `undated_note` each say what goes wrong AND why it matters; `empty_label`, `blank_input`, `empty_inputs`, `empty_note` and `no_shape` stop at the defect. A guard refusing on one of the short five leaves the reader knowing the entry is bad and not what a good one looks like. Levelling them up is prose work with a mechanical check available: a word floor per sentence, in the shape `PRIVACY_BODY_BASELINES` already uses for policy bodies.

- **`formatScannedFloorFailure`'s `invalid_floor` branch is the only one whose message comes from outside `FAILURE_MESSAGE`** — every other code renders from the failure's own fields; `invalid_floor` renders `f.detail ?? NUMERIC_FLOOR_DETAIL(f.minimum)`, so the sentence a guard prints was chosen by whichever of three call sites built the failure. The subject-joining refactor made those three call sites consistent, and left the mechanism that allows them to differ untouched. Carrying the problem CODE on the failure instead of a rendered string would let `FAILURE_MESSAGE` render it like the other five, and would make the fallback (a bogus minimum with no problem code) impossible to reach rather than merely unreached.

### Detail-accessor follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **`entryPatch` counts braces without knowing about strings** — the slice is correct today because every brace inside a note is balanced (`"parses to {}"`), which is luck rather than design. A note containing a lone `{` would run the count past the entry's close and the rewrite would land in a neighbour. A brace counter that skips over string literals is another ten lines; alternatively the fixture could assert the sliced entry ends where the NEXT key begins, which catches a mis-slice without teaching the test to lex TypeScript.

- **Nothing asserts that the ten patched entries are the only broken ones in the copy** — each case rewrites one entry, and the guard it runs reads only that entry, so a rewrite that accidentally corrupted a sibling would go unnoticed until some other case happened to run that sibling's guard. Running `validateScannedFloors` over the copy's table after a patch — one import from the copied module — would state "exactly one entry is broken, and it is this one" instead of leaving it to the case ordering.

- **The empty-input harness and this one now build fixtures from `SCANNED_FLOORS` in different directions** — `EMPTY_INPUT_FIXTURES` names inputs and copies siblings in; `CASES` names a check and rewrites its declaration. Both encode, per guard, which wrapper reads what and in which order it asserts. Neither reads the other's knowledge. A single `fixtureFacts(checkName)` derived from the table — its shape, its declared inputs, whether its count floor asserts before its input check — would let both files ask rather than remember, and would delete the standing "the fixtures encode assertion ORDER as a comment and nothing checks the comment" suggestion.

- **Ten wrapper runs of one module's validator take ~8s of the suite, and nine of them re-prove the plumbing** — every case pays a full loader boot to establish the same chain (guard starts → reads its entry → raises → prints one line). The variable part is the sentence, which is now pure. One wrapper run per SHAPE (count, inputs, delegation) plus a pure assertion per code would keep the end-to-end proof and drop most of the spawns; worth doing only if the file's wall clock starts mattering, and worth recording either way so the next reader knows the redundancy is deliberate.

### Ten-code harness follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **A delegated entry is never read by any wrapper, so its declaration is inert at runtime** — `check-privacy-baseline-provenance` is the only real `delegatedTo` in the table, and its guard asserts its premise in `lib/privacy-baseline-provenance.ts` without ever calling `scannedFloorFor`. So `empty_delegation` and `delegation_with_shape` on THAT entry can never fire in production; the new harness produces them through `check-appstore-config` instead, which is honest but sidesteps the question. Either the delegated wrapper should call `scannedFloorFor(CHECK_NAME)` for its own sake — one line, and it would make the table's claim about that guard enforceable where it is made — or the table should say out loud that a delegated entry is validated only by the test suite, so the next reader does not assume a runtime check that is not there.

- **`validateScannedFloors(SCANNED_FLOORS)` runs in a test and never in a guard** — `scannedFloorFor` validates the ONE entry it returns, so a broken entry for a guard that happens not to run (or that delegates) is caught only by `scanned-floor.test.ts`. `lint:all` runs twelve guards and could validate the whole table once, at the front, for the cost of a single call — turning "the table is sound" from something CI asserts into something the tooling refuses to proceed without. Worth weighing against the argument for the status quo: a guard should fail for its own reasons, not for a sibling's.

- **The ten cases assert the DETAIL sentence by re-typing a fragment of it** — `says: /not a positive integer/` is a copy of a clause in `PROBLEM_DETAIL`, which is not exported, so the two drift independently and a reworded sentence turns into a failing assertion in a file that has nothing to do with the rewording. Exporting `PROBLEM_DETAIL` (or a `detailFor(code)` accessor) would let each case assert `output.includes(detailFor(code))` — a stronger claim than a fragment match, and one that moves with the sentence.

- **Nothing asserts that a rewrite introduces exactly ONE problem, though the whole file depends on it** — the comment says it and the `says` assertions imply it, but a rewrite that tripped two codes would assert about whichever `validateScannedFloorEntry` reports first and would look green until the validator's declaration order changed. The check is cheap and pure: build the patched entry object in-process, run `validateScannedFloorEntry` on it, and require exactly one problem whose code is the case's key. That is a unit assertion beside a wrapper assertion, and it pins the premise the wrapper run cannot see.

- **`entryPatch` finds the entry's end with `indexOf("\n  },", start)` and would silently mis-slice a nested object** — no entry in `SCANNED_FLOORS` nests today, so the first `\n  },` after the key really is the entry's close; the day one grows a nested shape, the slice ends early, the rewrite lands in the wrong half, and `runPatched`'s no-op check may not catch it because the patch did change SOMETHING. Counting braces from the opening one costs about six lines and makes the fixture's parse a fact rather than an assumption about the table's current formatting.

### Shared-copy harness follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **Eight of ten `SCANNED_FLOORS` problem codes still have no wrapper run, and the copy that was blocking them is gone** — `validateScannedFloorEntry` reports ten ways an entry can be unusable and `lint-guard-invalid-floor.test.ts` runs two (`invalid_minimum`, `empty_inputs`). The reason to defer the rest was cost: one recursive copy per case. `runPatched` made a case two `writeFileSync` calls, so each remaining code is now a `patchedRun(label, from, to, checkName)` one-liner. `delegation_with_shape` is the one to run first — it is the only problem that means TWO places claim to own one premise, which is the exact shape that produces a guard everybody believes is covered.

- **Nothing asserts that the marker is on the copy's critical path rather than merely in the copy** — `makeSharedPatchedRepo` injects `console.error(PATCHED_REPO_MARKER)` into `lib/scanned-floor.ts` because every wrapper imports it, and that claim is load-bearing for every provenance assertion in the file. It is also a fact about twelve wrappers that nothing checks: a thirteenth guard that reached its premise some other way would print no marker, and its provenance assertion would fail with "the copy did not speak" when the truth is "this guard does not import the module the marker lives in". A registry-driven assertion — every `LINT_GUARDS` entry, run once out of a shared copy, must print the marker — would state the invariant where it is actually true.

- **`spawnSync` now captures stderr on green runs, and nothing yet reads it** — the channel was being discarded, which made "an ordinary run announces nothing about the override" vacuous; that one assertion is now real, and it is the only one. Every guard's PASS line, its premise note, and any warning it emits on the way to exiting 0 are newly visible to the harnesses and unexamined by them. The cheapest first use: assert that a guard exiting 0 prints NOTHING on stderr unless it was redirected — a guard that has started warning on every successful run is a thing the fleet should have to declare.

- **The empty-root suite is the last harness with its own idea of what a scan root is** — it was migrated onto the shared spawn but still builds its own `mkdtempSync` root and its own `after` cleanup, as do the partial-root, empty-input and invalid-floor files: four copies of the same six-line lifecycle, each leaking its roots if a fixture throws during construction rather than during a test. The standing `useScratchRoots()` suggestion now has one more caller and one fewer excuse; with the spawn already shared, the lifecycle is the only thing left that is four things.

- **`runPatched` re-applies and re-restores on a memo HIT, and the memo is the only reason that is cheap** — a cached run still walks the patch functions, rewrites the file, and writes the pristine bytes back before returning a result the spawn never produced. It is correct and it is a few KB of I/O per assertion, so it does not matter today; it matters the moment a patch function does something heavier than `String.replace`, or a fixture patches a file large enough for the write to be the cost. Checking the memo before applying — by exposing the key builder rather than duplicating it — makes the fast path actually fast and removes the only place where the fixture's behaviour depends on the cache being cheap.

### Restored-harness follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **`tsxLoader()` proved to be dead code in the one situation it exists for, and nothing would have told us** — the check throws a sentence naming `npm ci`, and until this run that throw happened inside the `execFileSync` argument list, where the surrounding `catch` rewrote it into `{ output: "", status: 1 }` — the exact empty-output refusal it was written to distinguish itself from. The ordering is fixed; the fact that a checkout with no `node_modules` was the only thing that revealed it is the finding. A suite that stubs `TSX_MODULE` to a path that does not exist and asserts `runGuardFrom` THROWS (rather than returning a captured failure) would pin the property, and would catch the next well-meaning refactor that folds the call back into the try.

- **The harness was removed on stale CI snapshots, and nothing in the repo distinguishes "job still running" from "poll is stale"** — the whole `[WIP]` round-trip cost a full task cycle because a check run reported `in_progress` at a frozen step timestamp long after it had finished, and the reader treated a repeated identical snapshot as evidence of a hang. The lesson is procedural, but it has a mechanical form: a note in `CONTRIBUTING`/`CLAUDE.md` saying that a check run is only evidence when its OWN timestamps have advanced between two reads — never on a single snapshot, never on two identical ones — would make the next reader spend a second poll instead of a revert.

- **Nothing asserts the guard-fixture memo key covers `scriptRoot`, and the copied-repo split is what made that necessary** — `runGuardIn` and `runGuardFrom` now share one `runs` map, and correctness depends on `scriptRoot` being part of the key: without it, the control copy and a patched copy of the SAME guard against the SAME scan root collide, the second read returns the first's result, and a green control would certify a patch that never ran. It is right today and it is one dropped template field from being silently wrong forever, because the wrong answer is a cache hit rather than an error. A two-line test — same guard, two different `scriptRoot`s, assert the statuses differ — costs one extra copy and makes the invariant a thing that fails rather than a thing that is remembered.

- **The negative control asserts the copy RUNS; nothing asserts the copy is the thing that ran** — `runGuardFrom(guard, control.root, REPO_ROOT)` passing proves a wrapper executed and exited 0, but a bug that resolved `guard.scriptPath` against `REPO_ROOT` instead of `scriptRoot` would produce exactly the same green, and would make every patched fixture in the file silently run the committed table. The patched cases catch it today only because they expect a refusal — which means the whole file's integrity rests on the patches, not on the control. Having the control patch print a marker (a `console.error` line the copy emits and the real checkout cannot) would let the control assert provenance rather than mere success.

- **Two of ten `SCANNED_FLOORS` problem codes have a wrapper run, and the copies are now the cost driver** — this pairs with the standing "copy once, patch per case" suggestion and raises its priority: each of the eight remaining codes adds a full `lib/` + `scripts/` copy at ~200 files, so covering them at the current shape is 8 more copies before a single new assertion is written. Doing the shared-copy refactor FIRST turns the remaining eight into `patchedTable(from, to)` one-liners over one fixture, which is the difference between a cheap follow-up and one that doubles the suite's wall clock.

### Per-input harness follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **Every fixture blanks its input; none of them proves the guard PASSES with all its inputs real** — the per-input split gives `check-sentry-version` two red runs, and both of them copy the other file in from this repository, so the "everything reads fine" configuration is built twice and never asserted on. One green run per guard against a fully-populated scratch root would pin the thing the red runs assume: that the copies are enough for the guard to get past its premise at all. Without it, a fixture whose `entries` copy silently produced nothing would still go red, for the wrong input, and read as a pass.

- **`makePartialRoot` copies `package.json` and `package-lock.json` per fixture and the lockfile is ~700KB** — the two `check-sentry-version` fixtures each copy the file the other one blanks, which is the correct shape and also the most expensive one in the harness by an order of magnitude. A fixture that wrote a MINIMAL real lockfile (`{"packages": {"node_modules/@sentry/react-native": {"version": "..."}}}`) instead of copying the committed one would be faster, would state exactly which keys the guard depends on, and would fail loudly if the guard grew a dependency on a third key — which copying the real file hides by definition.

- **The "exactly one blanked input" assertion reads `fixture.files` keys and cannot see a blanked input that arrived through `entries`** — copying in a file that happens to be empty in the repository would blank an input without appearing in `files`, and the assertion would pass while two inputs were empty. No such file exists today and the check is still worth having; making it read the scratch root after `makePartialRoot` (rather than the spec that built it) would close the gap and would also catch a `files` entry whose path does not land where the guard looks.

- **Four harness suites now each build their own `rootFor` + `fixtures[]` + `after(cleanup)` trio** — empty-root, partial-root, empty-input and invalid-floor all repeat the same six lines of scratch-root lifecycle, and each one leaks its roots if a fixture throws during construction rather than during a test. A `useScratchRoots()` helper in `guard-fixture.ts` returning a tracked factory would make the lifecycle one thing, and would give the leak-on-construction case one place to be fixed.

- **`EMPTY_INPUT_FIXTURES` now encodes, per input, which wrapper reads what — and `SCANNED_FLOORS` already declares that** — the fixture for `check-migration-docs` copies `supabase/migrations` because that wrapper's count floor asserts first; the fixture for `check-sentry-version` copies `package-lock.json` because it is the sibling input. Both facts are derivable: the count floor is in the table, and the siblings are the other names in `inputs`. A fixture builder that took only the input to blank and read the rest from `SCANNED_FLOORS` would shrink the map to a list of names and delete the standing "the fixtures encode assertion ORDER as a comment and nothing checks the comment" suggestion along with it.

### Patched-repo harness follow-ups (2026-08-13)

- **All six failure codes now have a red run, and `SCANNED_FLOORS` has ten problem codes with no run at all** — `validateScannedFloorEntry` reports ten ways an entry can be unusable; `lint-guard-invalid-floor.test.ts` runs a wrapper against exactly two of them (`invalid_minimum`, `empty_inputs`). The other eight are asserted only against hand-built fixture objects, which is the state `invalid_floor` itself was in yesterday: proven about a pure function, never proven about a guard. `delegation_with_shape` is the one worth running first — it is the only problem that means two places claim to own one premise, which is precisely the shape that produces a guard everybody believes is covered. The machinery is one `patchedTable(from, to)` call per case now, so this is a spec change rather than new harness.

- **`makePatchedRepo` copies `lib/` and `scripts/` for every fixture and the copies are never shared** — three fixtures in the new file, three full copies of ~200 files, and the negative control copies them a fourth time to prove the copy runs. Nothing is wrong with any single one; the total is the thing, and it grows linearly with every problem code the suggestion above adds a run for. A copy that is made ONCE and patched per-case (write the table, run, restore) would turn N copies into one, and the restore is the same `writeFileSync` the patch already does. Worth doing before the eight remaining codes each take a copy.

- **The patched-repo fixtures anchor on formatting inside `lib/scanned-floor.ts`, and the anchors are string literals in a test** — `'count: { label: "source file", minimum: 160 }'` is one prettier run away from not matching. `makePatchedRepo` refuses a no-op patch, so the failure is loud rather than silent, which is the important half — but the fix is loud in the wrong file: the reader is sent to a test to re-type a declaration that lives in a lib. Patching a JSON-shaped copy of the table (or letting the fixture write a whole replacement `SCANNED_FLOORS` block delimited by comment markers in the source) would make the anchor a thing the source declares rather than a thing the test remembers.

- **`formatScannedFloorProblem` exists for a caller that turned out to be a test** — `scannedFloorFor` builds its own sentence (`its SCANNED_FLOORS entry ${problem.detail}`) because the guard already prints its own name, so the only consumer of the `${checkName} ${detail}` shape is the registry assertion's `.map()`. Either the assertion should read the raw problems and the formatter should go, or the formatter should become the ONE place a problem turns into prose and `scannedFloorFor` should take its sentence from it with the subject as a parameter. Two ways to phrase one problem is how the two phrasings drift.

- **"Prints a refusal, not a crash" is the same regex re-typed in four harness files** — `assert.doesNotMatch(output, /\n\s+at\s/)` now appears in `lint-guard-empty-root`, `lint-guard-partial-root`, `lint-guard-empty-input` and `lint-guard-invalid-floor`, each with its own comment explaining it. It is the single most-repeated assertion in the guard harnesses and the one most likely to be subtly wrong in one copy (a stack whose first frame lands on line one of the output matches in none of them). An `assertNoStackTrace(output)` in `guard-fixture.ts` — where `tsxBinary()`'s ENOENT guard now lives — would make it one thing that four suites cite, and would give the "what a crash looks like" reasoning one home instead of four paraphrases.

### Empty-input harness follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **Five of six failure codes now have a red run through a wrapper; `invalid_floor` is the one left, and it is the only one that means the GUARD is broken** — `no_files`, `below_floor`, `empty_input` and `unreadable_input` are all produced by a fixture now. `invalid_floor` fires when a floor is below 1, which no entry in `SCANNED_FLOORS` is, so the only way to reach it through a wrapper is a table that a registry test already rejects. That is either a code path to delete or a check to move: the honest place for "this floor is bogus" is the registry test that reads the table, not a runtime branch in every guard, and today it is duplicated in both.

- **`check-sentry-version` declares two inputs and only the first one is ever named** — the fixture writes `{}` into both `package.json` and `package-lock.json`, `evaluateParsedInputs` returns on the first failure, and the assertion pins `package.json`. The lockfile half of the declaration has no run at all: a wrapper that stopped passing `package-lock.json` would fail the empty-input test on the other key and look covered. A second fixture with a real `package.json` and an empty lockfile is two lines and closes it, and the same shape applies to any future guard declaring more than one input.

- **The empty-input fixtures encode each wrapper's assertion ORDER as a comment, and nothing checks the comment** — `check-migration-docs` needs its migrations copied in because `assertScannedFloor` runs before `assertParsedInputs`, while `generate-powerbi-schema-doc` is the other way round. Swap either order and the fixture silently starts testing the other code, with a passing test and a stale comment. Standardising the order in the wrappers (inputs first — a guard that cannot read its inputs has nothing to count) would delete the whole distinction and make every fixture minimal.

- **`makePartialRoot` rejects an absolute literal path but not a traversing relative one** — `"/etc/passwd"` throws; `"../../etc/passwd"` resolves outside the scratch root and gets written. Nothing does that today and the fixtures are all hand-written, but it is a `path.resolve` and a `startsWith` check against the root, and the failure mode if it ever happens is a test that writes into the repository (or worse) while looking like it wrote into `os.tmpdir()`.

- **Three suites now spawn twelve guards each and the aggregate is uninstrumented** — `lint-guard-premise`, `lint-guard-empty-root`, `lint-guard-partial-root` and now `lint-guard-empty-input`, plus `lint:all` itself. Nobody knows what fraction of the suite's wall clock this is because nothing measures it; the number is quoted from memory in three different suggestions at three different values. Having `npm test` print per-file durations (node's test runner already emits `duration_ms` per suite) and pinning the guard files' share in one place would turn "the tests got slow" into a number that moves visibly.

### Partial-root harness follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **Two of the six floor failure codes have a red run; `empty_input` has never been produced by a wrapper at all** — the empty root covers `no_files`, the partial root now covers `below_floor`, and `unreadable_input` falls out of the empty root for the inputs-shaped guards. `empty_input` is the interesting one and it is only ever reached in a unit test of the pure function: it is the file that reads fine, parses fine, and carries `{}`, at which point `check-appstore-config` reports nine missing keys — a loud, correct-looking failure about the wrong thing. The fixture is one line on top of what now exists: a partial root holding an `app.json` whose content is `{}`, and the same for `package.json` under `check-sentry-version`. `makePartialRoot` copies real paths only, so it needs a sibling that writes a literal file into the scratch root.

- **Every partial fixture is far below its floor, so nothing tests the boundary the number actually defends** — `check-secrets` has a floor of 500 and its fixture hands it 19 files. That proves the code path fires; it says nothing about whether 500 is a number worth having, and a floor of 5 would pass the identical assertion. The interesting fixture is the one just UNDER the floor (copy everything except `lib/`, land near 550 against 500, expect green; drop one more root, expect red), which is also the only way to notice a floor that has drifted so far below the tree it can no longer catch losing a whole root. The helper takes a list of paths, so this is a spec change, not new machinery.

- **Three test files now spawn the same twelve guards, and `lint:all` spawns them a fourth time in the same CI job** — `lint-guard-premise` (green runs), `lint-guard-empty-root` (twelve red runs), `lint-guard-partial-root` (eight red runs plus nine single-root locks) and the aggregator itself. That is roughly forty `node --import tsx` boots, ~430 ms each, for work that is four passes over the same twelve scripts. The standing in-process suggestion (`export function main(argv, env)` per wrapper, `spawnSync` kept only for the exit-code assertions) has gone from "nice" to the single biggest lever on suite time, and each new harness makes it bigger.

- **`SINGLE_ROOT_LOCKS` re-declares each guard's scan roots in the test and pins them with a regex over the wrapper's source** — the wrappers spell the constant three different ways (`SCAN_ROOTS`, `SCANNED_DIRS`, `SCAN_DIRS`), so the test matches the array LITERAL rather than the name, which works and is string-matching where the registry could hold a fact. Adding `scanRoots` to the `LintGuard` interface (or to the `count` half of `SCANNED_FLOORS`) deletes the duplication, deletes the regex, and makes "this guard walks these roots" a type error to get wrong — the same move `checkName` is already suggested for one section down.

- **`makePartialRoot` will copy whatever it is handed, with no ceiling** — `fs.cpSync(..., { recursive: true, dereference: true })` on a spec that named `node_modules` or `dist` would write hundreds of megabytes into `os.tmpdir()` on a CI runner before a single assertion runs, and the symptom would be a slow test or a full disk rather than a message. A file-count ceiling in the helper (refuse a spec whose copy exceeds, say, 2000 files, naming the entry) turns a fixture mistake into the same kind of one-line refusal the guards themselves now give.

### Empty-root harness follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **An empty directory is the loudest failure, and `below_floor` is still hand-checked** — every guard now gets a red run, and all twelve of them fail on `no_files`, the code that was never the interesting one. The floor exists because a walk that lost `app/` but kept `lib/` reports a comfortable-looking number; that path still has no test. The fixture is one step further than the one that just landed: copy (or symlink) ONE scan root into the temp directory instead of leaving it empty, and assert `below_floor` with the count in the message. `check-inline-hex` (150 against 212, where `lib/` alone is well under) is the cheapest case to build it on.

- **`LINT_GUARD_REPO_ROOT` is a supported way to make any guard examine a stranger's tree** — the NOTE line is the whole mitigation, and nothing reads it. A guard pointed at another checkout that happens to pass prints a green line plus a note nobody greps for, in a CI log nobody opens on a green build. The history guard already went further for exactly this reason: with its override set it REFUSES to guess a base. The fleet-wide equivalent is refusing an override when `CI=true` unless a second variable opts in (`LINT_GUARD_ALLOW_ROOT_OVERRIDE=1`, set only by the test that needs it), which keeps the harness working and closes the "exported once at a workflow level" hole.

- **Three env vars now change what a guard checks and none of them is documented outside a doc comment** — `PRIVACY_BASELINE_BASE_REF`, `PRIVACY_BASELINE_REPO_ROOT` and now `LINT_GUARD_REPO_ROOT`. The repo keeps MANUAL-TASKS.md for "things a human must know before touching this", and the guards' own failure messages are still the only place these names appear to anyone not reading the source. One section, three entries, each saying what the variable moves and what it cannot make safe.

- **`guard-io`'s swallowing walk is now fleet-wide, so a partially-readable tree is quieter than it was** — `listDirEntries` returns `[]` for a directory it cannot read, per directory, which is exactly what the floor is a proxy for. Losing one small root (say `components/`) still leaves a count above every floor and a report that says nothing happened. Returning `{ entries, unreadable }` and failing on ANY unreadable directory is the stricter premise the count was standing in for, and it is now a one-file change instead of eleven.

- **Twenty-four more tsx spawns just joined the suite** — `lint-guard-empty-root.test.ts` runs twelve guards, `lint-guard-premise.test.ts` runs the same twelve again, and `lint:all` runs them a third time in the same CI job. Roughly twenty seconds of the run is now process startup for three passes over identical work. An in-process entry point (`export function main(argv, env)` per wrapper, called directly, with `spawnSync` kept only for the exit-code assertions) would collapse two of the three.

### Guard-premise follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **The premise test runs every guard against a tree where they all PASS, so it proves they report, not that they refuse** — thirty-eight assertions and every one reads a green run. The floor's whole value is in the failing case, and the failing case has still only ever been produced by hand-editing a script and reverting it. The scratch-checkout half of this task was dropped as too expensive (each guard needs `node_modules` to run); the cheap version is an env var per guard that overrides its scan root, the way `PRIVACY_BASELINE_REPO_ROOT` already does for the history guard, pointed at an empty directory under `os.tmpdir()`. One override, twelve reused, and every floor gets a red run in CI.

- **Twelve tsx spawns just landed in the unit suite, and nothing bounds what that costs** — the file takes roughly ten seconds against a suite that was already forty-eight, which is fine now and is the kind of thing that is discovered later as "the tests got slow". `lint:all` already runs all twelve in CI for real; the premise test re-runs them a second time in the same CI job for the assertions. Having `scripts/lint-all.ts` emit a machine-readable per-guard record (it already captures stdout for failures) and having the test read THAT would delete the second run entirely.

- **`numbersIn` accepts any number anywhere in the report, so a guard passes its floor on a number that is not a count** — `check-sentry-version` reports a version, `check-appstore-config` reports a key tally, and the assertion is `counts.some(n => n >= minimum)`. A guard whose walk collapsed to zero but whose message happens to carry an unrelated large number would pass. Having each guard print its count in a fixed, parseable form — `scanned=<n>` alongside the prose, or a `--json` mode — turns a loose regex over prose into a real reading.

- **The check name is derived by string surgery on `scriptPath` in three separate files now** — `.replace(/^scripts\//, "").replace(/\.ts$/, "")` appears in `scanned-floor.test.ts` twice and in `lint-guard-premise.test.ts` twice more, and it is load-bearing: it is the join key between the registry, the floor table and the guard's own log prefix. Add `checkName` to the `LintGuard` interface and the derivation disappears along with the class of bug where a script gets renamed and the floor silently stops being found.

- **`generate-powerbi-schema-doc` is the only registry entry whose script also has a non-guard mode, and the premise test runs it with `--check` only** — the same file rewrites the doc when run without the flag, and the count floor now sits above that branch too. The test never exercises the rewrite path, so nothing catches the case where the floor makes a legitimate regen impossible. Either split the guard out of the generator into its own `check-powerbi-doc.ts`, or run both modes in the test with the second one against a temp copy.

### Floor-fleet follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **Eight guards now assert a floor and not one of them has ever been SEEN to fail in CI** — every floor was mutation-checked by hand, locally, by editing the script and reverting it; nothing in the suite runs a guard against a deliberately-broken tree and asserts the exit code. That is the same gap the history guard had before `repoWithBase`, and it has the same fix: a scratch checkout with one scan root removed, the guard run against it, exit 1 and the message asserted. Eight hand-verifications that leave no artifact are eight things that quietly stop being true.

- **`check-clarity-input-mask` counts `.tsx` and its floor is shared with a walk that counts `.ts` too** — 45 for a 62-file walk and 45 for a 63-file walk was chosen to keep the numbers aligned, which is the kind of tidiness that stops being a measurement. The two walks differ by exactly one extension today; if `components/` grows a batch of `.ts` helpers the radius floor gets slack the clarity floor does not, and the alignment silently becomes a coincidence. Measure each independently and drop the "keeps the two floors aligned" note.

- **The migration floors are append-only, so they are wrong the moment they are written** — 18 against 25 migrations means the floor tolerates deleting seven files from a directory nothing is ever supposed to delete from. For an append-only input the honest floor is the CURRENT count, raised by the same commit that adds a migration, and the guard that would enforce that already exists in spirit: `check-migration-docs` reads the directory and MANUAL-TASKS.md together. Make the floor `>= documented count` instead of a slack constant and the number stops needing a human to remember it.

- **`generate-powerbi-schema-doc` asserts its premise in the generator path too, where it is not a guard at all** — the assertion sits above the `checkOnly` branch, so `npm run powerbi:schema-doc` (the REWRITE mode) also refuses to run when the taxonomy is small. That is defensible today and wrong in the one case that matters: deliberately shrinking the taxonomy now requires editing the floor before the regen that would justify the new number. Gate the count floor on `checkOnly` and leave the input assertion unconditional.

- **`CHECK_NAME` is now duplicated in twelve scripts and pinned by a regex in the test** — the test greps `const CHECK_NAME = "..."` out of each wrapper as text and compares it to a key derived from `scriptPath`. It works, and it is a string-matching stand-in for something the registry could own outright: add `checkName` to the `LintGuard` interface, have each wrapper import its own name from `LINT_GUARDS`, and the parity becomes a type error instead of a test that reads source files as strings.

### Scanned-floor follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **`evaluateScannedFiles` in `lib/bundle-premise.ts` is the same check with a weaker test, and it guards the bundle** — it fails only `count <= 0`, which is precisely the `no_files` half of the new floor; the `below_floor` half it lacks is the one that matters for `dist/`, where a partial export (Metro wrote one chunk before dying, a platform flag changed what got emitted) leaves a walk that finds SOME files and reports a clean secret scan over a fraction of the bundle. The two modules should not both exist in this shape: either `evaluateScannedFiles` delegates to `evaluateScannedFloor` with a bundle-file floor in `SCANNED_FLOORS`, or it keeps its own table and the shared helper grows the `REBUILD_HINT` variant it needs. Deciding which is a ten-minute call; leaving two near-identical premises is how one of them stops getting updated.

- **A floor measured once decays silently, and nothing says when it was last true** — `SCANNED_FLOORS` notes carry a date because the test demands one, but nothing compares that date, or the number beside it, against the tree it describes. `check-inline-hex` walks 212 files against a floor of 150; if the tree grows to 600 the floor is 25% of reality and stops catching the thing it exists for — a walk that lost two of three roots would pass. A second, LOOSE assertion in the other direction (fail when the actual count exceeds, say, 3× the floor, with the message "re-measure, this floor no longer describes the tree") turns quiet decay into a one-line chore. The registry test is the place; the number to compare against is already printed.

- **The floor lives beside the guard, not beside the walk, so a guard can still lie about what it counted** — `assertScannedFloor(CHECK_NAME, files.length)` trusts `files.length`, and every wrapper builds that array itself with a `catch { return }` swallowing unreadable directories per-directory. A walk that fails to read `app/` and succeeds on `lib/` reports the `lib/` count as if it were the whole scan; the floor catches the big version of that and not the version where one small root vanishes. Having `walkSources` return `{ files, unreadableDirs }` and failing on any unreadable directory is a stricter and simpler premise than a count — the count is a proxy for exactly this.

- **Nothing asserts the two wrappers' floors were measured from the SAME walk the wrapper performs** — the test reads both scripts as text and checks that `assertScannedFloor(CHECK_NAME, …)` appears; it cannot tell whether 150 was measured against `app+components+lib` or against something else entirely, because the measurement happened at a shell prompt and survives only as prose in a note. Running each guard once inside the registry test and asserting `scanned N` with `N >= minimum` would tie the number to a real execution — the same move the `repoWithBase` fixture made for the history guard, and the natural place is the third task of this section rather than a fourth.

- **`ScannedFloorError` is caught by an `instanceof` check in each wrapper, which breaks the moment tsx loads `lib/scanned-floor.ts` twice** — two module instances mean two distinct classes and an `instanceof` that answers false, at which point the wrapper rethrows and the guard fails with a stack trace instead of its one-line message. It has not happened (one tsx process, one resolution) but it is a fragile way to identify an error across a module boundary. A `code` string property on the error, checked instead of the class, is the version that cannot break, and it costs one line in each of the eleven wrappers this pattern is about to be copied into.

### Work-tree-premise follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **The other eleven guards never ask what they are pointed at either** — `--is-inside-work-tree` fixed one script, and the premise it now states is not special to this one: `check-secrets.ts`, `check-migration-docs.ts` and `check-bundle-size.ts` all walk a tree from `path.join(__dirname, "..")` and would report a clean pass over an empty directory, because zero files with zero findings is what a pass looks like from inside. A shared `assertRealCheckout()` — the work-tree probe plus a floor on how many files the walk found — would make "I looked at nothing" a failure everywhere the way it now is here, and the twelve-guard registry is the natural place to hang it.

- **Nothing fails a guard that examined ZERO of the thing it guards** — `evaluatePrivacyBaselineProvenance` already refuses `checked === 0` ("a pass over zero baselines is not a pass"), which is exactly the right instinct and exists in exactly one of the twelve. The others count what they found and report it without ever comparing that count against a floor, so a glob that stops matching (a rename, a moved directory, a tightened `.gitignore`) reads as a clean run. One asserted minimum per guard, in the guard, is a smaller change than the report line each of them already prints.

- **The shallow-clone failure and the work-tree failure should be one probe with two answers** — both say "history is not available here", both are checked in different places by different means, and only one of them is reached on the path where `--base` resolved. A single `describeRepository()` returning `absent` / `shallow` / `full` up front, asserted once, would collapse two special cases into one premise and make the third state (`full`) the thing everything below is allowed to assume. It would also let the report say which of the three it saw on every run, which is the input the standing "nobody measures how often the drift half skips" suggestion needs.

- **`--is-inside-work-tree` answers `true` inside a `.git` directory's parent and also inside a submodule or a nested worktree** — the guard now knows it is in *a* work tree and not that it is in *this* repository, so a `PRIVACY_BASELINE_REPO_ROOT` pointing at any unrelated checkout still passes the probe and proceeds to the explicit-base requirement. `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` compared against the root the script was handed would close the gap between "a repository" and "the repository", and costs one more spawn on a path that already spawns six.

- **A `MANUAL-TASKS.md` entry for the two env vars** — `PRIVACY_BASELINE_BASE_REF` and `PRIVACY_BASELINE_REPO_ROOT` both change what this guard compares, both are documented only in a doc comment inside the script, and one of them can turn the drift half into a comparison against a stranger. The repo already keeps a file for "things a human must know before touching this", and the guard's failure messages are currently the only place either name appears outside the source.

### Scratch-repository harness follow-ups (2026-08-12)

- **The harness that makes one guard testable is worth exactly eleven more** — `repoWithBase` is thirty lines (mkdtemp, `git init`, two commits, return the base sha) and it is what turned two unreachable branches into assertions.
