Twelve entries sit in `LINT_GUARDS` and the other eleven are tested the way this one was: by reading their source with a regex and asserting the right strings appear in it. `lib/check-migration-docs.ts` and `check-supabase-migration-naming.ts` are the next best candidates — both are pure functions over a file tree, so a scratch directory (no git needed) would let their failure paths be run rather than pattern-matched. Lifting the helper into `__tests__/` as a shared fixture is the precondition.

- **Six seconds of the suite is now process startup** — the seven scratch-repository cases each pay a full `node --import tsx` boot (~430 ms) to assert a handful of strings, and every future guard given the same treatment adds its own. Running the script once per FIXTURE rather than once per assertion is already done; the next lever is a single long-lived tsx process driven over stdin, or an in-process entry point (`main(argv, env)` exported and called directly, with `spawnSync` reserved for the two cases that genuinely need a separate process — the `PATH=""` one and the exit-code ones).

- **The `PATH=""` case proves ENOENT and not the signal death beside it** — `git()` treats `run.error !== undefined` and `run.status === null` as the same hard failure, and only the first has a test. The second is what a SIGKILL from an OOM killer or a container CPU limit looks like, which is a far more likely production shape than a missing binary, and the message it produces (`killed by SIGKILL`) has never been rendered. A fixture `git` shim earlier on PATH that kills itself would cover it, and would also be the mechanism for testing a git that returns garbage on stdout.

- **Nothing asserts the guard is still wired into `lint:all`** — this run made the guard's own behaviour thoroughly covered while its reachability rests on one registry entry, and the parity test only checks that each `lint:*` script is registered OR exempt, so deleting the entry AND the script passes. The same hole the `unparseable` work closed one level down, and the standing suggestion below says so for all twelve; the new end-to-end harness makes the honest version cheap — run `lint:all` in a scratch checkout and assert the guard's name appears in its output.

### Baseline-revision follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **The guard checks the table's provenance and not the guard's own presence** — `lint:baseline-provenance` is registered in `LINT_GUARDS`, and a commit that deletes the registry entry passes every check in the repo: the parity test only asserts that each `lint:*` script is registered OR exempt, and a removed script is neither. The same shape as the `unparseable` hole this run closed, one level up. Asserting the registry contains the entry (as `__tests__/privacy-baseline-provenance.test.ts` now does) covers the guard; nothing covers the other eleven.

- **`recordedOn` is compared as a string and never against reality** — `provenance_date_regressed` uses a lexicographic comparison, which is correct for `YYYY-MM-DD` and says nothing about whether the date is real: `2026-13-45` passes the format regex and orders fine. Parsing it (and rejecting a date in the future, which is how a copy-pasted placeholder shows up) would make the field a fact rather than a well-shaped string.

- **The note convention is enforced by the guard and documented nowhere a human reads first** — the failure message explains the `1171 → 980 (dropped the analytics section)` shape, which means the shape is learned by tripping over it. `MANUAL-TASKS.md` is where this repo puts "things a human must do"; a short entry on amending a policy file, naming the table and the convention, would move the discovery from a red build to the moment the policy is opened.

- **Nothing measures how often the drift half actually runs** — it now skips on a root commit, on a base predating the module, and (by design) never on a shallow clone, but the report prints those states and no one aggregates them. A one-line summary in the CI log is enough today; a guard whose skip rate nobody watches is the guard that has been skipping for a month.

### Baseline-provenance follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **Nothing enforces the note convention on the entry that needs it least and the one that needs it most equally** — `note_omits_previous_words` requires the new note to contain the old count, which is the right forcing function for a SHRINK and pure ceremony for a growth of two words after a typo fix. The check has both numbers in hand and could scale its demand: a change inside the ±25% band the drift gate already tolerates is ordinary wording work, while a change that would have gone RED is the one whose note a reviewer actually reads. Requiring a longer note (or a structured `reason:` field) only past the band would put the friction where the risk is.

- **A `MANUAL-TASKS.md` entry would close the loop the guard opens** — the guard fails the build and tells the committer to write a note, but nothing tells the person amending a policy that a baseline exists until CI stops them. The repo already has a documented place for "things a human must do" and a lint guard (`lint:migration-docs`) that enforces one such convention by file; a short "amending a policy file" section, plus the same style of check over policy files, would move the discovery from a red build to the moment the file is opened.

- **The five translations still move independently, and the provenance now makes that legible** — a change to the shared Sentry disclosure ought to move all five translated baselines in one commit, and today each is checked only against its own previous value. With `recordedOn` recorded per entry, the cheap version is now available: assert the five non-English entries share a `recordedOn` whenever any of them changes, so a half-finished re-translation that updated `de` and left `es` at the old size is a failure rather than four files quietly disagreeing about which disclosure they translate.

- **The guard reads the working tree, so CI and a local run can disagree about what changed** — `changedFilesSince` diffs the base against the WORKING TREE, deliberately, so an uncommitted baseline edit is caught while the note is easiest to add. The cost is that a dirty tree makes the guard's answer depend on unstaged work: a developer who has a policy file open for unrelated reasons satisfies `policy_unchanged` without committing anything. Reporting whether the tree was dirty (one `git status --porcelain`) would make the difference visible in the log, and is the precondition for ever tightening the check to `<base>..HEAD` on CI.

### Size-baseline follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **Character counts would make the six numbers comparable** — `de` renders the same disclosure in 169 words and `es` in 229, because German compounds what Spanish spells out, so one tolerance means six different amounts of real slack and the same 25% band is tighter on `de` than on any other file. Measuring characters (or calibrating a per-language multiplier off the current set) makes the band mean the same thing everywhere, and would let the tolerance be tightened without the shortest file setting the limit for all six.

- **Nothing checks the tolerance against the gap it has to fit into** — 25% was chosen against "no single section survives being dropped", which is a claim about the current documents that no test states. The English policy has a countable number of `<h2>` sections; asserting that the smallest of them is larger than 25% of the body would make the tolerance a measured fact the way the 0.9 similarity threshold is, instead of a number in a comment.

- **The five translations move together and the guard treats them as independent** — they are all the same Sentry disclosure, so a change to the disclosure ought to move all five baselines in one commit. One file drifting alone is a stronger signal than any of them drifting, and the per-file band cannot see it. Comparing each translation's ratio-to-its-baseline against the OTHERS' would catch a single half-finished re-translation that stayed inside its own band.

- **`missing_baseline` can never fire on a real build, which makes it a dead gate that reads as live** — `PRIVACY_PAGE_TARGETS` is derived from `PRIVACY_PAGE_LANGUAGES` and a test pins the baseline table's key set against that same list, so by the time a seventh language reaches `dist/` the suite has already gone red. That is the right outcome and it means the runtime code is unreachable by construction — worth either saying so in the comment (it is defence in depth against a caller passing its own target list) or moving the check to where a caller can actually trip it.

### Word-count-reporting follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **Emit the counts as data, not only as prose** — `formatPrivacyPageWordCounts` renders `169-1171 words` for a human and `wordCounts` is dropped on the floor by the CLI. The `--json` suggestion below now has two things to carry (per-page word counts and, once the ratio work lands, per-page similarity), which is enough payload to make a machine-readable mode worth building rather than a nice idea.

- **The range hides the middle four pages** — min and max are two of six numbers, so `es` drifting from 229 to 175 moves nothing in the printed line as long as `de` stays at 169 and `en` at 1171. Printing all six (`en 1171, es 229, pl 188, be 185, ru 182, de 169`) is one more line in a log that already prints two, and it is the difference between "the extremes are unchanged" and "every page is where it was".

- **Count the ENGLISH page against its own history, separately from the translations** — the five translations are Sentry-disclosure subsets and the English file is the canonical full policy, so they are not members of one population: 1171 and 169 are both correct and a range spanning them is nearly uninformative — it is dominated by a gap that is *supposed* to be there, and the five translations it hides sit inside 169-229, a band 25× tighter. Splitting the report into `en 1171 words, translations 169-229` states the document relationship the whole check is built on and makes each half's drift legible on its own scale.

- **Give `evaluateBundleSmoke` the same treatment** — the privacy half now reports what it measured and the bundle half still prints `15 token(s) found across 2 chunk(s)`, which counts what it was *asked* about, not what it *found*. Chunk sizes are the number that would move on a dependency bloat or a Metro config change, they are already in hand (the script reads every chunk's text to search it), and the guard is the only place in the build that could notice a 280 kB chunk becoming 2 MB.

- **Give the canonical English policy a committed baseline size** — every content gate compares against `PRIVACY.md`, and the only thing under that file is the same 40-word floor as everything else. A bad merge that cut it from 1171 words to 200 passes every check, and then the ratio and the section comparisons all run against a document that lost most of itself — quietly, because the translations would still look different from it. A tracked expected word count, updated deliberately when the policy is amended, is the one guard the baseline file does not have.

- **Word counts are not comparable across the six languages** — the shipped set already shows it: `de` renders the same Sentry disclosure in 169 words and `es` in 229, because German compounds what Spanish spells out. One floor for all six therefore means six different amounts of slack, and any future per-language length check (the "much shorter than the English disclosure" suggestion below) inherits the same distortion. A character count, or a per-language multiplier calibrated on the current files, measures the thing both checks actually mean.

- **Assert each `PRIVACY.md.<code>` has exactly one `# ` heading** — `extractPrivacyPageBodyText` cuts at the FIRST `</h1>`, which is correct only while each source file has exactly one top-level heading. A second `# ` line folds that heading's text into the body, where it silently inflates the word count and joins the similarity comparison. Nothing states the premise and nothing checks it; one regex per file in the suite would keep the extractor's contract true rather than merely currently satisfied.

- **Have `build-spa-fallback.ts` refuse to WRITE a page that fails the content gates** — the gates live in a separate post-build guard, so a truncated or untranslated source file is rendered, written to `dist/`, and only then reported, three minutes and one script later. `lib/bundle-smoke.ts` is node-pure and already imported by the build's own module; calling `evaluatePrivacyPage` on each page before writing it would put the failure at the point of the mistake and keep a broken disclosure off disk entirely. The post-build guard stays either way — it is the only thing proving the file on disk is the file that was rendered.

### Near-copy threshold follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **An all-pairs ratio needs a per-pair threshold, not the 0.9 constant** — the standing suggestion to compare translations against each OTHER (a `PRIVACY.md.be` copied from `PRIVACY.md.ru` passes today, since it differs from English) now has a measure to do it with, and reusing 0.9 unchanged would be wrong in both directions: `be` and `ru` share a large amount of vocabulary legitimately and already sit at 0.280 against each other — the highest pair in the set — while `de`/`es` sit near 0.2. A single constant either misses the copy it was written for or fails a real pair. A per-pair floor derived from the CURRENT measured matrix with slack (which is how 0.9 was chosen) is the same technique applied honestly.

- **Weight the ratio by rarity, so the words a policy cannot avoid stop counting** — the 0.157–0.280 that real translations score against English is almost entirely proper nouns, URLs, the effective date and product names: the tokens that survive translation by definition. Dropping words present in ALL six bodies before measuring would push real pairs toward 0 and leave copies at 1.0, widening a margin that is currently 0.28-to-0.9 and would become roughly 0.05-to-0.9. That matters most for the shortest translations, where a handful of shared proper nouns is a larger share of the body.

- **Carry the six measured ratios into the check's output** — the guard computes a number per page and prints a verdict, so nobody sees 0.28 drifting to 0.45 until the day it crosses 0.9 and the build goes red with no history to read. Pairs naturally with the standing `--json` mode suggestion for `check-bundle-smoke`: emitting the per-page ratio alongside the chunk count would let a future step diff them across runs, and would make the threshold's margin an observed fact on every build rather than a number measured once and written into a comment.

- **Move the prose gates off the build and into `npm test`** — `untranslated_heading`, `untranslated_body` and `near_english_body` all read rendered pages, but everything they need is a tracked `PRIVACY.md.<code>` and `renderPrivacyPage`, both of which the suite already imports and already runs over all six languages. Today the failure arrives from `lint:bundle-smoke` after a ~3-minute `expo export` in `verify`'s last leg; the same failure is available in the second leg in milliseconds. Keeping the dist-time copy is still right — it is the only thing proving the page that landed on disk is the page that was rendered — but the content questions do not have to wait for a build to be asked.

### Untranslated-body follow-ups (2026-08-11)

- **Assert each translation is a SUBSET of the English policy's section set, by count** — the five translated pages are Sentry-disclosure subsets of the canonical English document, and nothing states that relationship in code, so a translation with MORE `<h2>` sections than the English policy (a section invented in one language, or an English section pasted in twice) reads as fine. Counting rendered `<h2>`s per page and requiring the translations to sit at or below the English count is one number per page and encodes the document relationship the whole set is built on.

- **Reuse the body extractor to fail a page whose body is mostly the language picker** — `extractPrivacyPageBodyText` cuts at `</h1>`, which is correct for the current template and silently wrong if the picker is ever moved below the heading: the check would then compare six chrome-identical strings and pass everything. Asserting the extracted body does NOT contain every language label (the picker's fingerprint) would make that refactor fail loudly instead of hollowing the gate out.

- **Report all prose failures for a page, not the first one** — `evaluatePrivacyPages` deliberately stops at one failure per page so a wholly-English translation reports `untranslated_heading` rather than two rows saying the same thing. That is the right default and it hides a real distinction: with three or more comparisons (body similarity, section count, script range) the reader wants to know a page failed two of them, because one failing check is a mistake and three is a file that was never written. Collecting the codes and rendering `de: untranslated_heading, untranslated_body` on one line keeps the log short and stops discarding the signal.

- **Fail a translation whose rendered body is much SHORTER than the English disclosure section it mirrors** — every content check so far asks whether the text differs; none asks whether there is enough of it. A `PRIVACY.md.pl` truncated by a bad merge to its first paragraph differs from English, differs from the other translations, has a heading and a body, and passes everything. The five files are within a predictable band of each other today, so a floor derived from the shortest current translation (with slack) would catch the truncation class the whole guard still cannot see.

- **Compare each translation against the OTHER translations too, not only against English** — the baseline is the English page, so `PRIVACY.md.be` copied from `PRIVACY.md.ru` passes: it differs from English, which is all that is asked. Belarusian and Russian are the pair most likely to be copied and the pair a non-speaking reviewer is least likely to catch, so this is not a theoretical ordering of the language list. All-pairs over six headings is fifteen comparisons of strings already in memory.

- **Assert `<html lang>` matches the language the TEXT is in, using the script range** — `wrong_language` proves the attribute, `untranslated_heading` proves the text is not English, and neither proves the text is the language it claims. `PRIVACY.md.de` holding the Spanish policy passes both. A cheap script-range assertion (`ru`/`be` headings must contain Cyrillic; `de`/`es`/`pl`/`en` must not) is not language detection and does not pretend to be, but it separates the two alphabets the app ships and would have caught a be/ru swap.

- **Give the `PRIVACY.md.<code>` set its own tracked-file test, so a missing translation fails `npm test` and not only the build** — `build-spa-fallback.ts` exits 1 when a file is absent, which means the feedback arrives after a ~3-minute `expo export` in the `verify` chain's last leg. `PRIVACY_PAGE_LANGUAGES` is importable from a test, the files are tracked, and `fs.existsSync` per non-English code would move that failure into the second leg where the loop already reads the source tree.

- **Check the effective date line agrees across all six policy files** — every translation carries its own `_Effective date: …_` / `_Gültig ab: …_` line, currently 2026-07-04 in all six by hand. When the English policy is amended and the date bumped, five files keep the old one and the disclosure states a different effective date per language, which is the kind of inconsistency a regulator reads as two policies. The date is a single ISO string per file and grepping it is one regex.

### Translated-privacy-page follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Fail the build when `PRIVACY.md.<code>` drifts from `PRIVACY.md`'s section structure** — the English policy is the canonical document and the five translations are Sentry-disclosure subsets; nothing checks that a section added to the English policy reaches them. `__tests__/privacy-policy.test.ts` already guards the English file against the app's behaviour, so the shape to copy exists — the missing half is cross-file, and it is the failure mode a legal review actually cares about.

- **Serve the six pages through `scripts/serve-dist.js` in a smoke test that follows the language picker links** — the picker's hrefs are relative (`../`, `de/`) and were reasoned about for GitHub Pages' baseUrl, but nothing walks them. A test that starts the existing static server, fetches `/privacy/`, and follows every picker link expecting 200 would catch a relative-href regression that the per-file checks structurally cannot see.

- **Give `PRIVACY_PAGE_BODY_MARKER` a length floor rather than only a presence check** — `<h1` proves the markdown renderer ran; it does not prove it rendered more than a heading. A truncated `PRIVACY.md.pl` (a bad merge, a partial write) ships as a title and one line. A minimum rendered-body length, loose enough not to cry wolf, would cost one comparison in a function that already holds the text.

- **Check the `Content-Security-Policy` meta survives on all six pages** — `PRIVACY_PAGE_CSP` is strict by design (`default-src 'none'`, no scripts) and is the reason the page is safe to serve from the same origin as the app. It is emitted by the template, so it is exactly the kind of invariant that breaks silently in a refactor and is never noticed. One more token in the per-page gate list.

### Bundle-smoke follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Derive the watched provider list from `app/_layout.tsx` instead of restating it** — `BUNDLE_SMOKE_PROVIDERS` names four providers and a test asserts each is mounted, which catches a REMOVED provider and not an ADDED one: a fifth provider wired into the tree is never smoke-checked, and the check exists precisely because a provider silently dropping out of the bundle is the failure it was written for. The parity test already parses the layout; making the list the parsed set (with an explicit opt-out for any provider deliberately not watched) would make the coverage grow with the app.

- **Give `check-bundle-smoke` a `--json` mode so the CI log carries the numbers, not just a verdict** — it prints `15 token(s) found across 2 chunk(s)`, which is the right summary for a human and useless to anything downstream. A machine-readable mode would let a future step assert the chunk count did not jump (a Metro config change silently splitting the bundle into 20 chunks is invisible today) and would let the token report be attached to a PR comment rather than read out of a log.

- **Fail the smoke check when a watched i18n key appears in the bundle but with no translation body** — `grep`-for-the-key proves the key survived tree-shaking, not that it resolves to copy. A key present in the type but absent from a locale's table ships as a raw key on screen in that language, which is the exact class the six-language check was added for and which the key check does not reach. Matching `key:"` (a non-empty value) rather than `key` would cost nothing and close the gap.

- **Run the post-build guards over the DEPLOYED artifact, not only the locally exported one** — `deploy.yml` now runs `lint:bundle-smoke` before `upload-pages-artifact`, which is the right place, but the artifact is then stripped of sourcemaps and published, and nothing checks the thing GitHub Pages actually serves. A post-deploy step fetching `https://1337ninja1337.github.io/collectables/` and asserting the shell loads (plus `/privacy` returning 200) would catch the failure class the local checks structurally cannot: a correct bundle published wrong.

### `verify` gate follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Have `verify` print one line saying which legs it ran and that all of them passed** — the gate is one command now, but its output is still four tools' output concatenated, and the only summary is the shell exit code. A run that ends in a 4.33 MB bundle listing looks identical whether the 4626 tests ran or `lint:ci` was edited to skip them. A `scripts/verify.ts` that shells the legs in order and ends with `verify: 4/4 legs green (typecheck, guards, 4626 tests, build)` would make the claim a task entry recites machine-generated rather than hand-copied — and would make a skipped leg visible at the bottom of the log where the reader already is.

- **Give `verify` a `--changed` fast path for the tight loop** — the full gate is ~3 minutes, dominated by `expo export`; that is right before a commit and wrong for the fifth iteration on one suite, which is why `test:only` exists and why a run under time pressure reaches for the individual legs (the habit `verify` was added to remove). `npm run verify -- --changed` skipping the build when `git diff --name-only` touches no file the bundle contains (nothing under `app/`, `components/`, `lib/`, `assets/`, no `package.json`) would keep the one-command shape while making the common case seconds. Needs care: the skip must be LOUD, or it becomes a way to report a green build that never ran.

- **Fail `verify` when the working tree is dirty in a way the gate did not see** — the legs read the tree, not the index, so a run can be green while the commit that follows contains a file the typecheck never loaded (a `git add -p` that stages half a change, an untracked file the build would resolve). The gate's whole claim is "green here means green on CI", and CI checks out the COMMIT. Ending `verify` with a `git status --porcelain` diff against the files it read would close the last gap between the two — and it is the only remaining way to get a green verify and a red CI without changing any code.

- **Collapse ci.yml's four gate steps into `npm run verify` behind one composite action, or write down why not** — the local gate and the CI job are now two orderings of the same four commands, kept in sync by a test that compares them. That test is the right guard, but it is guarding a duplication that does not have to exist: a composite action running `verify` and keeping the per-leg annotations (via `::group::` markers) would give CI the same readable output with one definition. Filed rather than done because the per-step timing data in the Actions UI is genuinely useful for spotting a leg that got slow, and losing it may not be worth the dedup.

### `test:sentry` follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Derive the runner flags from one constant instead of asserting two scripts match** — `test`, `test:only` and `test:sentry` now share `tsx --import ./__tests__/test-globals.ts --test`, kept in sync by two assertions (byte-parity for the first pair, glob-masked parity for the third). That works, but the next script to join the family has to remember to add itself to a test. A `scripts/run-suites.ts` taking the glob as an argument would make the flags a single expression, and the three npm scripts one word apart each — at the cost of one process layer, which is why it is a suggestion rather than a fix.

- **Run the SDK-absent job against the OTHER absent-dependency cases the same premise implies** — the job proves the sentry surface survives `rm -rf node_modules/@sentry`, which was written for one crash. The same argument applies to `posthog-js` / `posthog-react-native` (both are lazy-imported behind the analytics façade for identical reasons) and to `@supabase/realtime-js`. A matrix over the scope to delete plus the glob to run would cost one job definition and would catch the next accidental static import of a deliberately-optional dep.

### Bundle-premise follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Assert the bundle premise's own freshness assumption against the CI clock** — the staleness comparison trusts mtimes, and `actions/checkout` stamps every file with the checkout time, which is what makes "dist/ is newer than the tree" hold on CI. That is an environment property, not a repo property: a future workflow that restores a cached `dist/`, or a self-hosted runner with a preserved workspace, would flip it and fail every guard for a reason none of their messages describe. One CI step printing the two mtimes the collector saw (or a `--explain` flag on the guards) would turn that into a five-second diagnosis.

- **Give `evaluateBundlePremise` a maximum-age check, not only a relative one** — it compares `dist/` against the source tree, which catches "you edited something after building". It does not catch "this bundle is from last Tuesday and nothing has changed since", which is the shape a deploy from a stale workspace takes. A `maxAgeMs` bound (loose — hours, not minutes) would close the one remaining way to check a bundle nobody meant to check, and it costs one more comparison in a function that already has the timestamp.

- **Fold the three post-build guards into one `npm run verify:bundle` entry point** — `lint:secrets:bundle`, `lint:bundle-size` and `verify:bundle-inlining` now share a premise, an input list and a failure vocabulary; what they do not share is an invocation, so ci.yml runs three steps that each pay the same walk and each collect the same chunk list. One script calling all three with the premise asserted once would make the walk happen once, and would give the post-build phase the same single-command shape `verify` gave the pre-commit phase.

- **Scan `dist/` sourcemaps for secrets even though they never ship** — `check-bundle-secrets` already walks `.map` files, but `lint:bundle-size` excludes them from the budget and `deploy.yml` strips them from the Pages artifact, so nothing states whether a credential in a sourcemap is a finding or noise. It IS a finding — the deploy workflow uploads those maps to Sentry, where they are readable by anyone on the org — and the current behaviour is right by accident rather than by decision. Worth one comment naming Sentry as the reason, and a test pinning `.map` in `SCAN_EXTENSIONS`.

### Vacuity-guard follow-ups (2026-08-10)

- **Move the sentry step's shell body into a script the suite can execute** — the guard was verified by extracting the step out of the YAML with a parser written for the occasion and running it; that is the right verification and the wrong home for it, because nothing re-runs it. A `scripts/ci/sentry-sdk-absent.sh` invoked by the job would let a test assert its behaviour directly (run it twice, expect the second to exit 1) instead of regex-matching YAML for the presence of the checks.

- **Assert the sentry glob's own test count, now that the job is the most exposed spot for a silent drop** — it reports 259 and nothing watches the number, and this job deliberately has no typecheck, so a suite that fails to LOAD there reports one failure instead of its N cases with no earlier step to catch the cause. Carried over from the previous block because closing the vacuity hole makes it the largest remaining gap in the same job.

- **Teach `ci-node-version.test.ts`'s workflow classifier to also require an npm script** — it already parses every workflow, splits comments from executable lines, and decides which files invoke the test runner. "Every suite invocation is reachable from a package.json script" is now true repo-wide but is enforced by one assertion inside a sentry-specific file, which is not where the next workflow author will look. The classifier is the natural home and needs no new parsing.

- **Give the `test` job the same treatment its own premise deserves** — it caches `~/.expo` and `node_modules/.cache` keyed on `package-lock.json`, and a restore-keys hit from an unrelated lockfile can seed a stale Metro cache into a build whose output four later steps then assert against. Nothing in the job reports which key was hit, so a cache-shaped failure is currently indistinguishable from a code-shaped one in the log.

- **Give the sentry glob its own floor, or fold the suites into the main run's count** — the job reports 256 tests today and nothing watches that number; a suite that fails to LOAD reports one failure instead of its N cases, and this job has no typecheck to catch the load error first. It is the single most exposed spot in CI for the count-drop signature the repo has now been bitten by twice.

- **Say in `CLAUDE.md` that a new CI job running suites must call an npm script** — the property "every suite invocation is reachable from a package.json script" is now true repo-wide and is enforced by one assertion inside a sentry-specific test file, which is not where anyone adding a workflow will look. One line in the Commands section (and, better, a check in `ci-node-version.test.ts`'s existing per-workflow classifier, which already parses every workflow for runner invocations) would make it discoverable and general.

- **Cache the typecheck so `pretest` is closer to free** — `tsc --noEmit` re-walks the whole program on every `npm test`, ~5s. `tsc --noEmit --incremental` with a gitignored `.tsbuildinfo` cuts a no-source-change run to well under a second, which matters because the escape hatch (`test:only`) exists purely to dodge that 5s — and an escape hatch nobody needs is one nobody can accidentally commit behind. Check that `expo/tsconfig.base` does not already set `composite`/`incremental` in a conflicting way first.

- **Make the `sentry-sdk-absent` CI job typecheck too, or state why it does not need to** — it runs `npx tsx --test __tests__/sentry-*.test.ts` directly rather than through `npm test`, so it is the one remaining path that executes suites with no type checking at all. It is covered in practice (the main `test` job typechecks the same files), but the asymmetry is invisible from the job definition, and the next hand-rolled `npx tsx --test` job will copy it. Either add a `npm run typecheck` step or a comment naming the job that covers it.

- **Fail the build when a `.test.ts` sits outside the runner's glob, from the lint registry rather than from a test** — `test-typecheck-prelude.test.ts` now walks `__tests__/` and fails on a nested suite the one-level glob would never execute, which is the right check in the wrong place: it only runs if the runner runs, and the failure mode it guards is "the runner silently ran fewer files". As a `scripts/check-test-glob.ts` entry in `LINT_GUARDS` it would run under `lint:all` — before the suites — and would join the aggregator's report instead of hiding inside a 4610-case run.

- **Assert the total test COUNT against a committed floor** — the Node 20 outage and the `tsx` type-stripping bug share a signature: coverage evaporated while the report still looked small and green (a suite that fails to load reports ONE failure instead of its N cases). A committed `MINIMUM_TEST_COUNT` checked by the CI step, bumped deliberately when suites are added, turns "the count quietly dropped by 55" into a named failure. It is the cheapest possible guard against the whole class and nothing currently watches it.

### Date-range validation follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Normalise the date bounds INTO the draft on blur rather than only inside the matcher** — `parseDateBound` pads `2024-1-1` to `2024-01-01` for comparison, but the field, `filters.dateFrom` and any future export/deep-link still carry the unpadded spelling. Two users with the same intent therefore persist two different strings, and a URL-shared filter round-trips a bound whose validity depends on a parser the receiving code might not call. Writing the normalised value back on blur makes the stored state canonical, and makes "what you see is what is matched" literally true.

- **Extract `<RangeFields>` from the two ranges the sheet now declares twice** — price and date are the same 30-line shape: a `fieldRow` with two `fieldHalf` inputs, a label each, a placeholder each, an `onChangeText` writing one draft key each, and an error slot below reading one exhaustive i18n record. They differ in four values. A `<RangeFields labels placeholders error onChange>` would halve the sheet's JSX and make the THIRD range (a cost-per-item band, a "listed between" filter on the marketplace) a five-line call instead of another copy — which is also the point at which the copy-paste starts binding the wrong draft key.

- **Fold the two validators into one `validateRange(from, to, parse)` over the shared parser shape** — `validatePriceRange` and `validateDateRange` are now byte-for-byte identical apart from the parser they call and the two error names they return; both walk `from` → `to` → compare, both prefer a parse failure over the inversion it would imply, and both were written by hand to have that precedence. A generic taking a `(raw) => {value, error}` parser and returning `{end, reason} | "inverted"` would make the precedence a property of ONE function, and the two i18n records could then key off `{end, reason}` instead of duplicating the five-code union each.

- **Reject a date bound in the future for `acquiredAt`, or at least warn on it** — an acquisition date cannot be later than today, so `dateFrom: "2027-01-01"` is a range that can only ever match nothing. That is the same "empty collection, no explanation" failure the inversion check was added for, just reached by a different route, and the check is one comparison against a clock. Filed rather than done because it needs a decision the pure module cannot make on its own: `lib/item-filters.ts` is deliberately clock-free (a `Date.now()` inside it would make the sort/filter memo non-deterministic under test), so the "today" bound has to be passed in by the caller.

- **Sanitize the price fields on keystroke instead of only validating on Apply** — `lib/format-currency-input.ts` already exports `sanitizeCurrencyInput`, which the create/edit cost fields use to strip everything but digits and one dot as the user types. The filter sheet does not, so `"$12"` is typeable, reaches the validator and is rejected with a message, when it could simply never have existed. Wiring `onChangeText={(v) => setDraft({ ...draft, priceFrom: sanitizeCurrencyInput(v) })}` would make `"unparseable"` nearly unreachable through the sheet and leave the validator guarding only the range inversion and whatever legacy values are already persisted — which is the right division of labour between a mask and a validator.

- **Surface which FIELD is at fault, not just which message** — the sheet renders one inline line under a two-column row, so `"“Price to” must be a number"` makes the reader map the message back to a field. `validatePriceRange` already knows the answer (the codes are `from_*`/`to_*`), so a `priceFieldError(error): "priceFrom" | "priceTo" | "both" | null` derived from the same union would let the offending input take a `DANGER`-bordered style. Cheap, and it removes the one place where the error text has to name a field in prose — which is also the only reason the four new strings need quotation marks around a UI label, a construction that translates badly.

- **Fold `parsePriceBound` and `parseCurrencyValueDetailed` into one parser with a domain parameter** — the two now share a normalisation step (comma → dot), a gate order (empty → unparseable → out-of-domain) and a deliberately-aligned error vocabulary, and the alignment is held together by a doc comment on each side. They differ in exactly one predicate: `n <= 0` for a listing price, `n < 0` for a filter bound. `parseAmount(raw, { min })` returning the shared shape would make that difference a call-site argument instead of a convention two files have to keep remembering, and would give the app one place to add thousands-separator handling when a user with a German keyboard types `"1.234,50"` — which BOTH parsers currently read as `1.234`.

- **Assert the disabled-CTA pattern once, in a shared test helper, now that three surfaces use it** — the filter sheet's Apply, `<DangerSection>`'s action and the settings delete button all express "unavailable" as reduced opacity plus a `disabled` prop, and only the first asserts that the accessibility tree agrees (`accessibilityState.disabled`). The other two dim a button a screen reader still offers. A `assertDisabledControl(node)` in `__tests__/helpers/` checking the prop, the a11y state and a non-default opacity together would make the next disabled control's test one line, and would catch the two existing gaps on the way in.

- **Give the sheet a "discard changes?" affordance rather than relying on stickiness alone** — the draft now survives a backdrop tap, which fixes the loss but leaves no way to abandon an edit on purpose: the only routes out are Apply (commit) and Reset (wipe every filter, including ones the user wants). A Cancel that reverts `draft` to `filters` would complete the triad, and it is the same two lines the old `openModal` used to run in the wrong place.

- **Extract the sheet's field rows into a `<FilterField label>` wrapper** — the sheet declares the identical `<View style={styles.field}><Text style={styles.fieldLabel}>…</Text><MaskedTextInput …/></View>` shape five times (price from/to, date from/to, source), differing only in label, placeholder and the `draft` key. A small wrapper plus a `bindDraft("priceFrom")` helper would remove ~40 LOC and make the next field a one-liner instead of a copy-paste that can silently bind to the wrong key — a bug no current test would catch, since each field is only ever read back through the key it was written with.

- **Assert the six-locale copy of the sheet through the harness, not just key parity** — `analytics-language-roundtrip.test.ts` proves `t()` returns different strings per language; what nothing checks is that the SHEET renders them, i.e. that no label in the filter sheet was hardcoded. Mounting `<ItemFilterBar>` inside `<I18nProvider>` per locale and asserting no rendered `#text` node repeats across two different languages would catch a hardcoded Russian string the moment it lands. Cheap now that both halves exist.

- **Fold the `beforeEach` AsyncStorage reset into the global test bootstrap** — `analytics-language-roundtrip.test.ts` clears the stub's module-scope store itself, and every future suite that mounts a persistence-backed provider will need the same line. `__tests__/test-globals.ts` already exists precisely for module-cache resets that outgrew per-file `beforeEach` hooks, and the stub is loaded lazily, so the same `require.cache` peek it uses for the realtime client applies unchanged.

### StrictMode harness follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Sweep the provider tree for the same double-mount class now that it is testable** — `<AnalyticsProvider>` was the one provider anybody suspected, but `SocialProvider`, `CollectionsProvider` and `ChatProvider` all open realtime channels in mount effects, and a channel subscribed twice is a duplicated message stream rather than a duplicated metric. Each is one `render(createElement(StrictMode, …))` away from an answer, with the sibling contexts mocked the way the analytics suite does it.

- **Fold `mockModule` into a `mockContexts({ auth, i18n, premium, diagnostics })` convenience** — five `mockModule` calls plus five module-scope `let`s is a lot of ceremony before the first assertion, and every provider suite will repeat it verbatim. A helper that takes an object of initial values and returns setters (`setUser`, `setLanguage`) would cut the preamble to two lines and stop the next suite inventing its own mutable-state convention.

- **Make the harness throw on a hook called after the render pass** — `press()` and `rerender()` re-enter the dispatcher correctly, but a stray hook call from an effect callback or an async continuation silently allocates a cell against whichever instance happens to be current, producing a wrong result rather than an error. The dispatcher already tracks `current`; asserting it is non-null outside a pass (rather than only inside `cell()`) makes the failure immediate and named.

- **Add an `unmount()` to `RenderResult` so cleanup effects can be asserted** — StrictMode's remount exercises cleanups, but a plain unmount (the sign-out path, a screen pop) does not exist in the harness at all, so `scheduler.dispose()`, channel teardown and subscription removal are only ever tested via the strict path. `unmount()` running every mounted effect's cleanup in reverse order is ~10 lines and closes the last lifecycle gap.

- **Pin the harness's own contract against a React upgrade in CI** — the dispatcher writes to `__CLIENT_INTERNALS_DO_NOT_USE_OR_WARN_USERS_THEY_CANNOT_UPGRADE.H` and the walker matches `Symbol.for("react.transitional.element")`, both of which a React major can rename. `render-harness.test.ts` would fail loudly, but only if someone runs it — adding a `lint:react-internals` guard that asserts the internals key and the element symbol still exist would put the breakage in the same report as every other code-style guard, next to the react version bump that caused it.

### React render harness follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Retire the two `[needs-dev-dep]` analytics mount tests off the harness instead of a dev-dep** — `analytics-provider-strict-mode` and `analytics-language-roundtrip` have been parked for a month waiting on a jsdom / react-test-renderer decision that no longer has to be made. Both are now writable: `<I18nProvider>` already mounts under `render()` (the `<FilterBar />` suite wraps every case in one), and the language round-trip needs only a `setLanguage` press plus a `trackEvent` spy. The StrictMode one needs one harness addition — a `doubleInvoke` render option that calls each component twice per pass — which is ~5 lines given the dispatcher already owns the hook store.

- **Give `render()` a `providers` option so a test can inject a context value without mounting the real provider** — every `<ItemFilterBar>` case pays for a full `<I18nProvider>` mount (an AsyncStorage read, a `useMemo` over the 2000-line translation table) purely to get `t()`. `render(element, { providers: [[I18nContext, fakeI18n]] })` would make a case that does not care about copy an order of magnitude cheaper, and would let a test drive a hard-to-reach provider state (`ready: false`, a locale with no translation) without reaching through the provider's own effects. Needs `I18nContext` exported from `lib/i18n-context.tsx`, which is a one-word change.

- **Convert the highest-value source-text scans into runtime assertions, starting with the style-array ones** — `link-aschild-style-flatten.test.ts` regex-scans for `<Pressable style={[…]}>` under a `<Link asChild>` because a raw array style crashed the deployed web app (`Failed to set an indexed property [0]`). That is now directly observable: mount `<ItemCard>` and assert the node's `style` prop is not an array. A regex cannot tell a two-entry array from a conditional that only ever produces one object; the render can. Same for `item-cost-render.test.ts`'s "withLabel in BOTH branches" count.

- **Add a `<Modal>`-aware `openSheet()` helper to the harness** — the filter sheet, the wishlist sheet and the share sheet are all only reachable by pressing a trigger and re-rendering, and each future test will re-derive "find the button, press it, re-query". A `tree.press(...)` + assertion that `Modal.props.visible` flipped is two lines, but the sheet's contents then need a fresh query against the new tree; a helper that does the press and returns the mounted sheet subtree would keep those tests to the assertion that matters. Pairs with the `<Modal visible={false}>` rule the walker already implements.

- **Make the react-native stub fail loudly on an unimplemented export instead of returning `undefined`** — the stub exports a hand-picked list gathered by grepping the app's current imports. The first component to import something absent (`Keyboard`, `AccessibilityInfo`, `PixelRatio`) gets `undefined` and a confusing downstream `TypeError` several frames away from the cause. Wrapping the module namespace in a `Proxy` whose `get` throws `unimplemented react-native export: X — add it to __tests__/helpers/stubs/react-native.mjs` turns a ten-minute debug into a one-line message. The `.mjs` namespace object is frozen, so this means exporting a proxied `default` and keeping named exports for the common ones.

### `<DangerSection>` follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Land the three consumers the extraction was filed for** — collection delete (`app/collection/[id].tsx`), profile delete (`app/profile/[id].tsx`, admin-only) and premium cancel (`app/settings.tsx`'s `handleCancelPremium`) all render destructive actions with their own inline styling today. The widget is one line per call site now, so the actual work is deciding each one's tone: premium cancel is reversible (`"soft"`), the two deletes are not (`"hard"`). A third and fourth consumer is also what proves `tone` is the right axis — two can always be explained by coincidence.

- **Fold the confirm-dialog boilerplate into the component** — every destructive call site repeats the same 15 lines: a `Platform.OS === "web"` branch calling `globalThis.confirm`, an `Alert.alert` branch with a cancel + `style: "destructive"` button, and a `void performX()` thunk. `lib/confirm-dialog.ts` already exists. An optional `confirmTitle` / `confirmMessage` pair on `<DangerSection>` would let the component own the round trip, which would also guarantee no future destructive action ships without a confirm step — the failure mode nobody notices until a user loses data.

- **Give the "soft" tone a busy state distinct from disabled** — `disabled` currently means both "you may not press this" and "this is in flight", both rendered as `opacity: 0.6`. Settings' delete button already distinguishes them in its label (`deleteAccountDeleting`), which is a hint the component should express: a `busy` prop that shows a spinner would remove the need for every caller to build a ternary label, and would stop a slow delete looking identical to a permanently-disabled button.

- **Pin the `<DangerSection>` recipe against `<SoftDestructiveChip />` before both exist** — the item-filters suggestions block proposes a `<SoftDestructiveChip />` for the `CARD_BG_10` + `DANGER_SOFT_2` + `DANGER_DEEP_4` pill, which is *exactly* the token trio `<DangerSection tone="soft">` now owns; the only difference is the chip is small and carries an icon. Shipping both would put the same three tokens in two components and re-open the drift the extraction closed. Better to add `size?: "sm" | "md"` and an optional `icon` here and delete the other suggestion.

- **Widen the "no file inlines these hex values" sweep into a reusable test helper** — `danger-section.test.ts` walks all of `app/**` and `components/**` asserting none of its nine hex values appears anywhere, which is a stronger guarantee than the per-file adoption tests the migration program has been writing one at a time. `hero-banner.test.ts` hand-rolls the same `walk()`. A shared `__tests__/helpers/ui-files.ts` exporting the walk plus an `assertNoInlineValues(tokens)` helper would make that pin cheap enough for every future extraction to include.

### `COOL_*` family follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Give `COOL_GRAY_LIGHT` and `COOL_GRAY_DARK` their first real consumers on the disabled-control surfaces** — the axis exists but only `COOL_GRAY` is used (the settings diagnostics off-toggle). The obvious adopters are the disabled states that currently borrow warm tokens: `app/people.tsx`'s pagination (`pageButtonDisabled` = `BORDER_4`, `pageButtonTextDisabled` = `MUTED_16`) and the item-filters apply button. Those read as "faintly branded" rather than "inert" today, which is exactly the confusion the family was added to fix. Filed rather than done because recolouring live controls is a visual change that wants a designer's eye, not a mechanical sweep.

- **Fold the three copies of the YIQ `luminance()` helper into one test module** — `design-tokens-cool-family.test.ts` and `toast-design-tokens.test.ts` now each declare the same six-line function, and the `DANGER_*` / `MUTED_*` family assertions still filed in this document would make a fourth. A `__tests__/helpers/luminance.ts` (or an exported helper in `lib/design-tokens.ts`, which would also let the app use it for contrast decisions) removes the drift risk before the copies diverge on rounding.

- **Extend the "minimum perceptible step" assertion to every numbered colour family** — the cool family now asserts adjacent shades differ by >20 YIQ units, which is what stops a future `COOL_GRAY_2` landing 4 units from its neighbour: invisible on screen, monotonic on paper, passing the ordering test. The warm families are where this actually bites — `MUTED_22` (`#6b4d35`) and `MUTED_23` (`#6a4d35`) differ by one unit in the red channel and are almost certainly an accidental duplicate rather than two intended shades.

- **Audit `MUTED_22` / `MUTED_23` (and the rest of the 29-token `MUTED_*` run) for accidental near-duplicates** — one-channel-apart pairs mean two call sites that look identical will drift apart on the next rebrand, and a 29-deep numbered family is past the point where a contributor can pick the right shade by reading the list. A script that clusters the palette by perceptual distance and reports pairs under a threshold would turn this from an eyeball task into a list, and would feed the family-consolidation work directly.

- **Add a `theme.neutral` entry to `components/use-app-theme.tsx` backed by the cool axis** — every other surface colour reaches the screens through the theme hook, but the cool family is imported as static tokens, so a future dark mode has no way to swap `COOL_GRAY` for a shade that works on a dark ground. Threading it through the hook now, while the family has exactly one consumer, is a one-line change; doing it after the disabled-state adoption above is a sweep.

### Toast design-tokens follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Ship the `__tests__/design-tokens-family-luminance.test.ts` the toast migration hand-rolled half of** — `toast-design-tokens.test.ts` now carries its own YIQ luminance helper to assert `SUCCESS_SOFT_2 > SUCCESS_SOFT > SUCCESS_GREEN_3 > SUCCESS_DEEP` and the matching `DANGER_*` chain. That helper is the third place this idea has been filed (see the settings and wishlist suggestion blocks) and the first place it actually exists. Hoisting it into a shared test module and running it over `DANGER_*`, `SUCCESS_*`, `MUTED_*`, `HERO_DARK_*` and `CARD_BG_*` would turn the suffix numbering from a convention into an invariant — and would immediately flag the families where it does not hold today, which is the useful part.

- **Retire the historical token aliases the uniqueness check had to be scoped around** — `TEXT_ON_DARK` and `CARD_BG_2` are both `#fff7ef`, which is why the new "no value aliases an existing token" assertion covers only the nine tokens the migration added. Two names for one hex means a rebrand that edits one and not the other silently splits a surface that used to match. A sweep that picks the semantically-correct name per call site, deletes the loser and then widens the assertion to the whole palette is mechanical; the only judgement needed is which name each site meant.

- **Give the toast palette a semantic indirection layer (`TOAST_PALETTES` in `lib/design-tokens.ts`)** — the 3×4 table still lives in `lib/toast-context.tsx`, so the tokens are named but their *grouping* is not. Moving the table next to the palette (as `TAG_COLORS` already is) would let a future "dark mode" or "high contrast" variant swap one exported object rather than editing the provider, and would let the adoption test assert on data instead of matching source with a regex.

- **Wire the toast into `useAppTheme()` like the rest of the app** — every screen now reads `theme.card` / `theme.border` / `theme.text` from `components/use-app-theme.tsx`, but the toast host resolves its colours from static tokens at module scope. On a dark background the success and info toasts render pale-on-pale. This is the last provider-level surface that ignores the theme, and it is the reason the migration produced static tokens rather than theme entries.

- **Add a "hex literal reintroduced" guard for the four files still on `HEX_ALLOWLIST`** — the allowlist is down to `design-tokens.ts`, `placeholder-color.ts`, `privacy-page.ts` and `export-pdf.ts`, all of which legitimately produce colour values. But the exemption is per-file, not per-region: a new colour added anywhere in `lib/export-pdf.ts` outside its HTML template is invisible to the lint. Narrowing each exemption to the specific declaration (a `// lint:hex-allow` comment scoped to the next block, say) would shrink the blind spot to what actually needs it, and the existing "every allowlist entry actually carries hex literals" test shows the surface is already being watched.

### Recent-items selector follow-ups (2026-08-09)

- **Route `components/search-overlay.tsx` through `selectOwnedActiveItems` too** — its `matchedItems` memo already checks `isWishlist` and `archivedAt` by hand, but it never checks collection ownership: it filters on `item.createdByUserId === ownerFilter` only when an owner filter is active, so with no filter set a search on the home overlay can surface items from collections merely shared with or followed by the user. Whether that is a bug or the intent is a product call (search *may* want to span shared collections), which is why it is filed rather than fixed — but the two filters should not silently disagree. If shared items are wanted, the ownership gate belongs behind an explicit `scope: "mine" | "all"` argument on the shared helper.

- **Give `selectOwnedActiveItems` a `Map`-based sibling for the per-collection screens** — `app/collection/[id].tsx` and `components/item-card.tsx` still re-derive "does this item belong to a collection I own" one item at a time via `getCollectionById(...)?.role`. Each call is a linear scan of the merged collections array, so a 200-item collection performs 200 scans per render. Exporting a memoisable `collectionRoleIndex(collections): Map<string, CollectionRole>` next to `ownedCollectionIds` would turn those into O(1) lookups and give the contexts one canonical index to hand out.

- **Audit the other consumers of the flat `items` array for the same archived-items leak the stats screen had** — `app/stats.tsx` counted sold items toward `totalValue` for as long as `archivedAt` has existed, because it predated the field and nobody swept the call sites when it landed. `lib/collections-context.tsx` exposes `items` to at least six screens; a `scripts/check-item-filter-parity.ts` guard that flags any `items.filter(` whose predicate mentions `isWishlist` but not `archivedAt` would have caught it mechanically, and would catch the next field added to the exclusion set.

- **Make `RECENT_ITEMS_LIMIT` the cap for the rail's mounted images, not just its array length** — the limit bounds the array at 10, but all ten cards mount their remote `<Image>` immediately inside a horizontal `ScrollView`, which is the same up-front-mount pattern the collection screen needed chunked pagination to escape on iOS. The rail is small enough that this is a latent cost rather than a live one, but `<LazyPhoto>` already exists and the rail is its natural second adoption point.

- **Sort the rail on a real timestamp comparison instead of ISO string ordering** — `selectRecentItems` compares `createdAt` lexicographically, which is correct only while every value is a fixed-offset ISO string. `lib/supabase-row-coerce.ts` passes through whatever Postgres returns and the seed data is hand-written, so one `+03:00` offset or one `Date.toString()` value would silently mis-order the rail with no error anywhere. Either coerce at the boundary and pin it with a test, or compare `Date.parse` values with a NaN fallback.

### Pull-to-refresh spinner follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Collapse the seven identical `handleRefresh` bodies into a `useRefreshHandler(fn)` hook** — all seven screens now declare `const [refreshing, setRefreshing] = useState(false)`, the same six-line `setRefreshing(true); try { await fn() } finally { setRefreshing(false) }`, and the `useMinimumVisible(refreshing)` line. That is three concepts and one state variable repeated seven times; a hook returning `{ refreshing: showRefreshing, onRefresh }` would make each screen one line and make the hold impossible to forget on the eighth screen. The reason it is filed rather than done: two of the seven bodies do extra work inside the `try` (re-fetching a specific collection / item), so the hook needs to take an async thunk and the call sites need care.

- **Guard the hook-after-early-return trap tree-wide, not just in `app/collection/[id].tsx`** — that file has a bespoke `collection-detail-hook-order.test.ts` because it crashed once, and it caught a real regression during this task. Every other screen is unguarded, and `app/profile/[id].tsx` and `app/item/[id].tsx` have the same shape (several early returns, a long hook preamble). The existing test's logic is already file-agnostic; pointing it at every `app/**/*.tsx` would generalise it for roughly the cost of a loop.

- **Give `<Screen>` the hold instead of every caller** — `useMinimumVisible` is applied at seven call sites that all feed the same `<Screen refreshing=… onRefresh=…>` pair. `<Screen>` could apply the hold itself and take the raw flag, which would cover the two `<FlatList>` cases only if they moved to it too. Worth weighing against the current explicitness: the hold is visible at the call site today, which is why the rejected-debounce test can assert on it.

- **Reconsider `MIN_SPINNER_VISIBLE_MS = 400` against the actual indicator** — the number is a reasoned guess at "one rotation", not a measurement. iOS and Android spin at different rates and react-native-web renders a third thing entirely. If any of the three completes a cycle meaningfully faster, the hold is padding a spinner that already read fine; if slower, 400ms still truncates it.

- **Add a "refresh failed" path** — all seven handlers swallow errors: `refresh()` rejecting lands in `finally`, the spinner retracts, and the screen looks identical to a successful no-op refresh. Now that the spinner is guaranteed visible for 400ms there is a natural slot for the opposite signal (a toast, or a one-line banner), and `lib/toast-context.tsx` already exists.

### `<DashboardBanner />` follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Give the row an `accessibilityRole="link"` and a spoken label that includes the hint** — today a screen reader reaches a `<Link asChild><Pressable>` with no role and no label, so it announces the three child `<Text>` nodes in sequence and the trailing "›" as a bare glyph. `<HeroBanner>` just gained `accessibilityRole="header"` on the same principle; the banner rows are the more consequential case because they are the navigation, not the decoration. The chevron should also be hidden from the accessibility tree with the paired iOS/Android props the search row already uses.

- **Land the marketplace "Sales" row the extraction was filed for** — the original task named it as the third consumer and it still does not exist. Now that the widget is one line per row, the actual work is the route and the two i18n keys. Worth doing while the shape is fresh, because a third consumer is what proves the tone axis is the right one (two consumers can always be explained by a coincidence).

- **Replace the "★" / "⊞" text glyphs with `Ionicons`** — every other icon in the app comes from `@expo/vector-icons`; these two are literal characters, which means they render in whatever face the platform picks for that codepoint and can differ visibly between iOS, Android and web. The component takes `icon: string` today, so the swap is one prop type change and two call sites.

- **Extend `lint:radius` to ban the bare `20` and `14` these copies inlined** — `RADIUS_CARD_SM = 20` and `SPACING_SECTION = 14` both already exist as tokens, and both banner copies inlined the raw numbers anyway. The guard's banned sets (`999` / `22` / `24` for radius, `10` / `12` / `8` for gap) simply do not include them, which is exactly why. This is the same finding the `<HeroBanner>` migration filed for `32` and `24` — four values, one one-line change to `GEOMETRY_RULES`.

- **Audit the remaining `padding: 16` literals for a missing token** — the banner's padding is the one geometry value in it that could NOT be tokenised, because the `SPACING_*` family jumps 14 → 20. `16` is a common enough card padding in this repo that its absence looks like an oversight rather than a decision; counting the sites would settle whether it deserves a `SPACING_COMFY` or whether those sites should move to 14 or 20.

### `<HeroBanner />` follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Fold `app/profile/[id].tsx`'s hero into the component as a third tone** — it is the one dark banner the extraction did NOT absorb, because its shape genuinely differs: solid `HERO_DARK`, `alignItems: "center"`, an avatar instead of an eyebrow, and an absolutely-positioned settings button. But it shares the radius, the padding, the fill and the `TEXT_ON_DARK_*` text colours, so it will drift away from the other eight the first time someone retunes the banner family. Either a `tone="portrait"` variant or an explicit "this is deliberately not a HeroBanner" assertion in `hero-banner.test.ts` would close the question; today it is open by omission, which is the state the eight copies were in.

- **Give `<HeroBanner />` a light-theme path now that `useAppTheme()` exists** — all eight consumers now render one component, which makes this a one-file change for the first time (it was previously eight). The banner is currently the only surface in the app that ignores the theme entirely: `HERO_DARK` and `TEXT_ON_DARK_3` are hardcoded while the cards directly beneath them read `theme.card` / `theme.text`. Pairs with the `useThemedGradient()` note already filed under the `lib/gradients.ts` follow-ups.

- **Extend `lint:radius` to ban the bare `32` that three copies inlined** — `borderRadius: 32` is `RADIUS_HERO_LG` spelled as a magic number, and the guard's banned set (`999` / `22` / `24`) does not include it, which is precisely why login, friends and the collections feed each shipped it. Adding `32 → RADIUS_HERO_LG` to `GEOMETRY_RULES` is a one-line change and would have caught all three at author time rather than at extraction time. Same argument for `padding: 24` → `SPACING_GUTTER`, which all eight copies inlined and only home did not.

- **Add a "no fontFamily-less Text style on a dark surface" audit** — the collections-feed headline rendered in the platform default face for as long as it existed, next to seven serif siblings, and nothing failed. A structural scan for style blocks that declare `color: TEXT_ON_DARK*` and a `fontSize` but no `fontFamily` would have named it in one line. The class is broader than the banner: every omitted `fontFamily` in this repo is invisible in review, invisible in git blame, and only visible on a device.

- **Pin the eight consumers' eyebrow strings against reuse** — three screens (people, friends, chats) all pass `t("community")` as their kicker, which is either intentional grouping or three screens that were copy-pasted and never re-labelled. Now that the call sites are one-liners the question is finally legible; a test that lists eyebrow-per-screen would make the answer explicit instead of leaving it to whoever next reads all eight files.

### `lib/gradients.ts` follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Land the `OVERLAY_*` family + `withAlpha(token, alpha)` and retire the scrim's raw `rgba()`** — this has now been filed from four different migrations (profile-detail, photo-preview, visibility-badge, search-overlay) and `PHOTO_SCRIM_GRADIENT` makes it five. The helper is ~5 lines over the existing hex tokens; the reason it keeps getting deferred is that each filing only saw its own 2-3 literals. Counted together the repo has well over a dozen, and `lint:hex` is blind to every one of them because they are `rgba()` and not `#rrggbb`.

- **Widen `lint:hex` to catch `rgba()` literals too** — the cover-photo scrim sat duplicated across two files precisely because the guard that exists to stop exactly this only matches `#rrggbb`. The same scanner with one more pattern would have flagged it the day the second copy landed, and would cover the `OVERLAY_*` literals above as a side effect.

- **Point the `<HeroBanner />` extraction at the gradient bundle** — that suggestion (still open, filed under the home-screen migration) describes the hero card's *chrome*: gradient + eyebrow + title + actions row. The gradient half is now done, which shrinks it to a layout-only extraction and removes the "where does the gradient live" question that made it feel bigger than it was.

- **Give `HERO_DARK_GRADIENT` a light-theme sibling now that `use-app-theme.ts` exists** — the three hero screens hardcode the dark banner regardless of theme, while everything around them reads from `useAppTheme()`. A `useThemedGradient()` that picks between two bundles would make the hero the last surface to respect the theme instead of the one that ignores it.

- **Pin the scrim's alpha ramp against a legibility floor** — the test asserts the gradient darkens downward, which is the direction but not the amount. White text over a 0.55-alpha scrim on a bright cover photo is marginal; a floor (bottom stop ≥ some alpha) would encode the actual requirement rather than its sign, and would catch a "soften the scrim" tweak that quietly makes titles unreadable on light photos.

### Placeholder-audit follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Promote the audit into a `lint:i18n-placeholders` guard** — it currently runs inside `npm test`, which means a translator editing `lib/i18n-context.tsx` in isolation only learns about a clipped hint if they happen to run the full 4249-case suite. The scan is already a pure function over source text with no React dependency, so lifting it into `scripts/` alongside `check-inline-hex.ts` and adding it to `lint-all.ts` is mechanical, and it makes the failure arrive in the guard block where the other structural rules already live.

- **Measure the single-line cap instead of guessing it** — 40 characters was chosen because every existing single-line placeholder happens to fit under it, not because 40 characters is what a 360dp field actually renders. The real budget depends on the font (`DMSans-Regular` at 14–15px) and the field's horizontal padding, both of which the design tokens already state. A tiny width estimate would replace a round number with the actual constraint, and would probably tighten it.

- **Extend the derive-from-JSX trick to `accessibilityLabel`** — the same scan shape (find the prop, walk out to the enclosing element) would let a test assert that every icon-only `Pressable` in the repo carries a label, which is the `lint:a11y-icon-labels` guard already suggested twice above and still unwritten. The placeholder audit is the working proof that the parse is reliable enough to gate CI on.

- **Cover the `label={t("…")}` keys with the same length discipline** — placeholders were audited because they clip, but a field LABEL that wraps to three lines on a narrow phone pushes the form's whole layout around, and nothing checks those today. Same scan, different prop, probably a different cap.

- **Fail the audit on a placeholder that duplicates its own field's label** — `t("sourceLabel")` / `t("sourcePlaceholder")` are distinct keys, but nothing stops a future translation from collapsing them into the same string, which wastes the hint entirely (the user is told twice what the field is and never what to type). The pairs are derivable: a `label` and a `placeholder` on the same element are already found together by this scan.

### `<SheetSearchRow>` follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Give `<SheetSearchRow>` a `trailing` slot so `components/search-overlay.tsx` can adopt it too** — the overlay is the fourth copy of this row and the only one this extraction could not absorb, purely because it renders a close-overlay button after the clear chip. One optional `ReactNode` slot (plus letting the caller bump `size` 18 → 20) would collapse the last hand-rolled copy, and its a11y contract — just re-implemented by hand — would stop being a thing anyone has to remember.

- **Write the `lint:a11y-icon-labels` guard against the shape this task just proved out** — the audit found unlabeled icon-only `Pressable`s in three files by reading them, and there is no reason to believe those were the last. The rule is now concrete enough to encode: a `<Pressable>` whose only child is an `<Ionicons>` needs an `accessibilityLabel`, and an `<Ionicons>` that is NOT inside a named Pressable needs both `accessibilityElementsHidden` and `importantForAccessibility="no"` — with the paired-props check, since shipping one of the two is the failure mode that keeps recurring.

- **Fold `app/people.tsx`'s search field into the shared row** — it is a page-level field rather than a sheet row (own card chrome, a visible sibling label, theme-driven colors), so it was fixed in place here. But it is still a magnifier-less `MaskedTextInput` with a hand-rolled style and no clear button: a user who typed a long profile id has to backspace it. Either give the shared row a `variant="page"` or accept the divergence deliberately and document it.

- **Add a live-filter path so the sheet's debounce hook finally has a consumer** — `useDebouncedValue` shipped two tasks ago and was deliberately NOT wired into `ItemFilterBar` because Apply reads `draft` directly. Now that the row is a component, a `debounceMs` prop that fires a second `onCommit` callback would let the collection filter as you type without touching Apply's semantics, which is the flow the debounce was built for.

- **Pin the row's placeholder/label pairs as a single i18n invariant** — there are now four `*A11y` keys whose only contract is "longer and fuller than the placeholder shown in the same field", each asserted by its own hand-written test. Deriving the pairs from a table in the test (`[placeholderKey, labelKey]`) would make the fifth consumer's keys checked automatically instead of relying on whoever adds them to also copy the assertions.

### Quick-chip row follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Extract `<ActiveFilterChip icon label onClear />` from the query and sort chips** — the two are now byte-for-byte the same shape (amber pill, leading icon, label, trailing close-circle, both icons hidden from the accessibility tree, a `*ChipClear` label key) differing only in which field they clear. A third narrowing worth surfacing — the price range, "with photos" — makes it three copies, which is one past the extraction threshold this repo has been holding to.

- **Surface the price range as a chip too** — it is the last active filter with no visible trace: a collection narrowed to €10–€50 looks identical to an unnarrowed one except for the "Filters (1)" badge. The formatting is the only real work (currency symbol, open-ended ranges like "under €50"), and `format-cost.ts` already owns it.

- **Give the quickChips ScrollView a scroll affordance** — the row now holds up to four chips plus the filter button, and on a narrow phone the reset chip sits past the fold with nothing indicating there is more to the right. A fade-out gradient on the trailing edge, or `showsHorizontalScrollIndicator` on web only, would signal it without costing layout.

- **Make the query chip open the sheet on long-press** — tapping clears, which is the common case, but a user who wants to *refine* "penn" to "penny" has to open the sheet and find the field. A long-press that opens the sheet with the search input focused turns the chip into the entry point for the flow it describes.

- **Pin the chip row against a "silently narrowed collection" regression as a single invariant** — there are now three independent chips each tested separately, and the property that actually matters is the one none of them states: every field `countActiveFilters` counts should have a visible surface. A test deriving the chip list from the counted fields would fail the moment a fourth filter is added without a chip, which is exactly how the query ended up invisible in the first place.

### Accessibility follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Sweep the codebase for the other icon-only `Pressable`s** — the filter sheet's clear button turned out to be an unlabeled tappable element, and it is unlikely to be the only one: the share sheet, the sort chips' ✕, the people-screen follow buttons and the item-card overflow menus all follow the same "icon inside a bare Pressable" shape. A one-off `tsx` scan for `<Pressable` blocks whose only child is an `<Ionicons`, minus the ones carrying an `accessibilityLabel`, would produce the actual work-list instead of guessing.

- **Add a `lint:a11y-icon-labels` guard once that list is known** — the pins added here name `components/item-filters.tsx` specifically, so a new unlabeled icon button anywhere else still passes green. The same scanner that produces the sweep list becomes the CI gate, in the shape `lint:hex` already established: an allowlist for the deliberate exceptions, a failure for everything new.

- **Pin the paired-platform-prop rule globally, not per file** — hiding a decorative icon needs BOTH `accessibilityElementsHidden` (iOS) and `importantForAccessibility="no"` (Android), and shipping one is a silent half-fix on the other platform. The count-parity assertion written here only covers one file; as a repo-wide rule it would catch the mistake at the moment someone makes it.

- **Give the sheet's Apply/Reset actions an `accessibilityHint`** — "Apply" alone does not convey that it closes the sheet and re-filters the collection, and "Reset" does not convey that it wipes every field including the query the user just typed. Two hint strings are cheap and prevent a destructive surprise for a non-visual user.

- **Decide whether `accessibilityRole="search"` is right for a TextInput on web** — React Native accepts it and TypeScript is happy, but on react-native-web it maps to ARIA `role="search"`, which is a landmark for the containing region rather than a role for the input itself. It may be more correct to put the role on the wrapping `sheetSearchRow` `View` and leave the input as a plain labeled textbox. Worth one pass with a real screen reader on web before copying the pattern into the next sheet.

### Debounce hook follow-ups (2026-08-08)

- **Ship the live-as-you-type path the hook was built for** — `useDebouncedValue` exists now, but `ItemFilterBar` still only applies its `query` when the user taps Apply, so the in-collection search costs a modal round trip per refinement. Wiring the search row's value straight into `onChange` through the hook (150ms) is the natural next step, and the reason the debounce had to land first: `applyItemFilters` over a thousand-item collection must not run per keystroke. Note the commit path must stay exact — Apply has to read the raw draft, not the settled one, or a fast tapper applies a stale needle.

- **Give `useDebouncedValue` a `flush()` escape hatch** — the moment live filtering lands, "type a needle then immediately hit Apply/Enter" needs a way to settle the pending value synchronously instead of waiting out the window. A `[settled, flush]` tuple (or an options `{ flushOn }`) keeps the commit path exact without every consumer re-deriving the raw value alongside the debounced one.

- **Add a leading-edge option for the empty-string reset** — clearing a search field currently waits the full 300ms before `searchResults` clears. It is invisible today because both consumers gate their render on the raw query, but that is an invariant nothing enforces.
